Client-Ready Engineer · 90-Day Upskilling Plan Page 2 of 198

=== PAGE 3 ===
1. Why this plan
Our business now focuses on one market: US healthcare. US healthcare clients expect the engineer on the call to
understand their business, speak clearly, own outcomes, and hold a position when it matters. On many projects
there will be no project manager between the engineer and the client.
Our engineers are technically strong. The gap is client-facing communication. These are the common starting points
we see across the team:
Hesitating to speak up on calls, especially with senior or fast-talking clients.
Answering only what was asked, instead of driving the conversation forward.
Giving updates only when someone asks — so others have to chase for status.
Freezing, guessing, or over-apologising when asked something we don't know.
Delaying bad news or disagreement until it is too late to fix calmly.
Not knowing how to open a call, set an agenda, or close with clear next steps.
None of these are personality problems — quiet, careful people often become the most trusted client-facing
engineers. They are skills, and skills improve with short, structured, daily practice. This plan provides that practice.
What "done" looks like on Day 90
The engineer can run a client call and the client relationship on their own:
1. Opens the call with a warm start and a clear agenda — the engineer leads, not the client.
2. Asks questions that uncover the real business need, and plays back what they heard.
3. Gives a status update in under 60 seconds, answer first.
4. Explains trade-oﬀs in business terms and makes a recommendation.
5. Handles "I don't know" calmly and follows up on time.
6. Raises risks early and delivers bad news with a plan.
7. Disagrees professionally when needed.
8. Closes with clear next steps and sends a written recap within 2 hours.
9. Keeps the client informed in writing without being asked.
Design principles
Principle Individual and self-paced Practise every day Prepare → practise → reflect Real work from week 2 Private practice, visible
progress
Zero cost Healthcare-ready What it means in practice
Each engineer follows the plan alone, Monday to Friday, 45+ minutes a day, plus optional
weekend practice (4–6 hours a week in total). No group sessions to schedule.
Every day, Monday to Friday, includes a 10–15 minute mock call with an AI "client"
just reading or videos.
— not
5 minutes of preparation before every call, a written recap after it, and a one-line log.
Writing drills use the engineer's real project updates. Phase 3 moves onto live client calls:
shadow, co-lead, then lead.
Recordings are private by default — an engineer shares one only if asked. Management
sees progress through a weekly scorecard, bi-weekly check-ins, and live phase gates.
Every activity can be done with free resources. Paid courses and books are listed as
optional upgrades.
Business-level understanding of US healthcare — HIPAA, EHR/FHIR, patient apps, billing
— is built into Phase 2.

2. The plan at a glance
90 days ≈ 13 weeks (Monday–Friday), plus optional weekend practice. Phase 3 has five weeks instead of four to
leave room for scheduling live client calls. The plan is three months: depending on the month, a phase can run a
week longer or shorter — that's fine.

=== PAGE 7 ===
4. How the plan works
The daily 45-minute block (Monday–Friday)
Minutes Block What you do
5 Prep 10 Learn 10–15 Mock 10 Write 5 Log Fill in a Pre-call card (Appendix A1) for today's mock: your goal, opener, 3 points, 3
questions, 1 risk, and closing line. Use AI to help (Appendix B3).
Today's concept: read the framework box in this plan, plus the listed article or video.
A voice call with an AI client on today's scenario (section 7). Treat it like a real call: you
open, you lead, you close.
Write the post-call recap, or today's writing drill. Paste the transcript into the AI and ask for
feedback against the call rubric (Appendix B2).
Write 1–2 lines in your practice journal: one thing that went well, one thing to fix tomorrow.
Fridays: add the weekly self-scorecard (5 minutes, section 5).
Every second Friday (Weeks 2, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12): a 15–20 minute check-in with your manager.
Phase 3: on days with a live client call, the real call (plus its prep and recap) replaces the AI mock.
Pick a fixed time that suits you — for example, early morning or evening. The habit matters more than the
length.
Increase diﬃculty each week. Level 1: friendly client. Level 2: busy and vague. Level 3: sceptical, interrupts,
pushes back.
Weekend (optional, 20–30 minutes): do the week's hardest mock card again, one level harder.
Setup — Day 1 (about 20 minutes, one time)
1. Install an AI app with voice mode on your phone or laptop — Claude, ChatGPT, or Gemini. The free plans are
enough.
2. Create a practice journal (a Google Doc or Notion page) with five sections: Pre-call cards · Recaps · Daily log ·
Weekly scorecards · Phrases I like.
3. Save the Master mock prompt (section 7) somewhere you can paste it in 10 seconds. (Every mock also has a
ready card with the full prompt — see "Start here" in section 7.)
4. Optional: set up a private recorder (phone voice memo or QuickTime) — see "Record for yourself" below.
5. Book the bi-weekly check-ins (Weeks 2, 6, 10, 12) and the three phase gates (end of Week 4, end of Week 8,
Day 90) with your manager now, so they are already in the calendar.
Record for yourself — private by default
Listening to yourself for two minutes is the fastest way to hear what a client hears: the long pause before the
answer, the "basically" at the start of every sentence, the moment you rushed. Recordings are private by
default. They are never required and never used to judge anyone. If your manager or coach asks, you can
choose to share one — or make a recording to share. Otherwise, delete them when you're done. Voice-mode
apps also save a text transcript, which is often enough on its own.
Only record a real client call if your project and the client allow it.
Client-Ready Engineer · 90-Day Upskilling Plan Page 7 of 198

=== PAGE 8 ===
Data safety — non-negotiable in healthcare
US health data is regulated (HIPAA). Never paste PHI — patient names, dates of birth, record numbers,
diagnoses, screenshots with patient data — or client credentials or confidential documents into any AI tool the
company hasn't approved. Mock scenarios use made-up or anonymised details. When in doubt, leave it out.
How to run an AI mock call (in one minute)
1. Open voice mode. Paste the Master mock prompt with today's persona, scenario, and diﬃculty filled in.
2. Say "Start the call.
" You speak first — open the call the way you would with a real client.
3. After 10–15 minutes, say "End the call. Give me feedback.
"
4. The AI scores you against the rubric and suggests three improvements. Note the one to focus on tomorrow.
5. Write the recap (or today's drill) and ask the AI to review it (Appendix B5).
Tip: ask the AI to speak at natural American speed, to cut in at your next pause if you ramble, and to ask at least
one question you can't answer. Comfort with that moment is the goal.
Client-Ready Engineer · 90-Day Upskilling Plan Page 8 of 198

5. Progress and management visibility
Four simple mechanisms give the engineer a clear view of their own progress and give management early visibility of
who is improving and who needs help. None of them require recordings (an engineer can share one if asked).
Management judges progress on live observation, written work, and the scorecard.
Mechanism When Who What management sees
Weekly self-scorecard Every Friday, 5 min Engineer Scores 1–5 per pillar with 1–2 lines of proof;
days completed

5.1 Weekly self-scorecard (Friday, 5 minutes)
Score (1–5) Proof — 1–2 lines: what I learned, what I struggled with
Pillar Understand
Communicate

Days completed:
/

5

Biggest win this week:
Where I'm stuck / what help I need:
Score Meaning
1 I avoid it, or I freeze when it happens.
2 I do it only when prompted, and it feels awkward.
3 I can do it when I have prepared.
4 I do it naturally on most calls.
5 I do it well under pressure — hard questions, an upset client, a senior audience.
Be honest. The scorecard exists to show where help is needed, not to grade anyone. A 2 with a clear "where I'm
stuck" line is more useful than a hopeful 4.
5.2 Bi-weekly manager check-in (15–20 minutes)
1. Score trend (3 min). Look at the last two scorecards together.
2. Live spot-check (2–3 min). The manager asks for one skill, live: "Give me your status update now"
, or one
surprise client question such as "What happens if the EHR vendor delays access by two weeks?"
3. Where are you stuck? (5 min).
4. Support needed (5 min). For example: a shadowing slot, a lighter week, a pairing session, a specific resource.

=== PAGE 12 ===
6. The call rubric
One rubric, used three ways: for the engineer's self-review, for the AI's feedback after every mock, and for
management at the gates. Score each criterion from 1 to 5. The descriptions for 1, 3 and 5 are anchors; 2 and 4 sit
between them.
# Criterion 1 — Needs work 3 — Solid 5 — Excellent
1 Opening and
agenda
Starts abruptly, or waits for
the client to lead
Greets the client, states the
purpose and agenda
Warm start, clear purpose, invites the
client's input, checks the time
2 Answer-first
structure
Background first, answer
buried
Answer first most of the time Always answer first, with 2–3 clear
points; concise
3 Questions asked Few or none; mostly yes/no Several open questions;
some follow-up
Uncovers the real goal and success
metric with a clear question funnel
4 Playback None Plays back at the end Plays back at key moments and
confirms understanding
5 Business link Only technical talk Sometimes mentions
business impact
Every key point tied to a client
outcome or metric
6 Trade-oﬀs Options with no
recommendation, or jargon
Clear options and a
recommendation
Simple, business-framed trade-oﬀ;
the client decides quickly
7 Handling
unknowns
Guesses, freezes, or over-
apologises
Admits it and commits to
follow up
Calm; shares what is known; gives a
clear follow-up time
8 Risks and bad
Hides or delays it Raises it when asked news
Raises it proactively, with impact,
options, and the next update time
9 Disagreement Silently agrees, or argues States the concern politely Acknowledges their goal, uses facts,
oﬀers an alternative, reaches
agreement
10 Closing and next
steps
Ends without next steps Lists the next steps Recaps decisions, owners and dates;
ends on time
11 Written follow-up None, or late Sent the same day Sent within 2 hours; bottom line first;
action items with owners and dates
Not every call tests every criterion — a demo may have no disagreement. Score only what came up, and average
those scores.

=== PAGE 13 ===
7. AI mock session kit
Every day includes a 10–15 minute mock call with an AI playing a US client. This section gives you everything you
need: one master prompt, five client personas, three diﬃculty levels, and a library of 49 scenarios mapped to the
weeks. The other prompts (prep, feedback, recap, writing review) are in Appendix B.
Start here: your first mock in 10 minutes
Every mock in the plan has a ready-to-run card, set in one practice project (CareBook, section 7.5). You don't
need to build a story — just practise.
1. Open this week's page (sections 8–10) and find today's scenario ID in the day table, e.g. S07.
2. Go to "Week N — mock cards" at the end of that week and find the card.
3. Prep (5 min): read page 1 of the card — the story, your fact sheet, and the example script. Fill in your Pre-
call card (Appendix A1).
4. Open Claude or ChatGPT, start a new chat, and paste the AI block from page 2 of the card as text. Don't
read its hidden facts.
5. Tap the voice icon and speak first, the way you would open a real call.
6. After 10–15 minutes, say "End the call.
" Read the feedback and note the one thing to fix next time.
7. Write (10 min): send yourself the recap (Appendix A3) and write your 1-line log.
More practice? Appendix E has 12 optional extra cards (X01–X12).
Before and after real client meetings, use the master prompts in Appendix B6 (prep) and B7 (meeting notes) —
a full worked example is in B8.
7.1 Master mock prompt
Paste this at the start of every mock. Fill in the four lines in brackets.
Master mock prompt — copy and paste
You are role-playing a US client on a video call with me. Stay in character until I say "End the
call.
"
WHO YOU ARE: [Persona, e.g.
"Dana Brooks, VP of Product at a telehealth startup in Austin, Texas"]
PROJECT CONTEXT: [2–3 lines about the project. Made-up or anonymised. No real patient data.]
TODAY'S SCENARIO: [Scenario from the library, e.g.
"S07 – You want 'a dashboard for your clinics'
but haven't thought through the details"]
DIFFICULTY: [Level 1 friendly / Level 2 busy and vague / Level 3 sceptical, interrupts, pushes back]
HOW TO BEHAVE
- Speak like a real American business person: natural speed, short sentences, everyday expressions.
- Don't volunteer information. Give details only when I ask good questions.
- If I use technical jargon, ask me what it means for your business.
- If I talk for more than 45 seconds without getting to the point, cut in at my next pause.
- Ask me at least one question I probably can't answer.
- Keep each of your turns short (1–3 sentences), like a real call.
WHEN I SAY "End the call"
, step out of character and give me feedback:
1. Score me 1–5 on each criterion that came up: opening and agenda, answer-first structure,
questions asked, playback, business link, trade-offs, handling unknowns, risks and bad news,
disagreement, closing and next steps.
2. Quote 2 things I said well, and 2 things I should have said differently — with the better
sentence.
3. Tell me roughly how often I used filler words ("um"
,
"basically"
,
"actually"
,
"like").
4. Give me ONE thing to focus on in my next call.

7.3 Diﬃculty levels
Level How the AI client behaves Use it in
Level 1 Friendly and patient. Answers your questions fully. Weeks 1–3, and any brand-
new skill
Level 2 Busy and vague. Gives short answers, changes topic, is slightly impatient. Weeks 2–8
Level 3 Sceptical. Interrupts, pushes back, asks hard questions, applies time pressure. Weeks 6–13, gates, dress
rehearsals
7.4 Scenario library
Every scenario has a full ready-to-run card at the end of its week: the story, your fact sheet (what's done, what's
delayed, what you need from the client), an example script, and the exact prompt to paste. Click an ID to jump to
its card, or go to "Week N — mock cards"
.
ID Scenario — and the situation you walk into S01 S02 S03 Intro call with a new client: introduce yourself and the project plan
It's Monday, March 2 — Day 1 of the plan. Until now our delivery manager ran
every client call; from today Dana hears from you. This is your baseline: read
the story and fact sheet (2 minutes), skip the script, and just do the call.
Rapid-fire status questions: "Where are we? When will it be done? Any
risks?"

=== PAGE 20 ===
7.5 Practice project: CareBook
Every mock card in this plan is set in one fictional project, so you learn one account deeply — its people, its
numbers, its history — the way you would on a real client. Use it for every mock, and for any activity that says "or
use the practice project" if you are not on a client project yet. All names, companies, dates and numbers are made
up.
Ready-to-paste project context
My company is the engineering partner of Brightpath Health, a US telehealth company. We are building
CareBook v2: a patient app and web portal where patients book in-person or video visits, get SMS reminders,
and fill in intake forms, and where clinic staﬀ manage schedules. CareBook syncs appointments with the
clinic's Epic EHR and checks insurance eligibility through Suncoast Billing. The launch customer is Lakeside
Health (12 primary-care clinics in Ohio); the pilot at 3 clinics is planned to go live on Monday, April 27. All
details are made up — no real patient data.
The project in one paragraph
Brightpath Health is a US telehealth company (HQ in Austin, Texas; engineering oﬃce in Boston) that sells
CareBook to physician groups. We are Brightpath's engineering partner and are building CareBook v2: a React
Native patient app, a React web portal for clinic staﬀ, and Node.js APIs on AWS. The launch customer is Lakeside
Health, 12 primary-care clinics in Ohio that use Epic. The first pilot is at 3 Lakeside clinics. After the pilot, CareBook
rolls out to all 12 clinics.

Decisions, blocker, risks and numbers
Item Details
Tech decision 1 Tech decision 2 Live blocker Risk 1 Risk 2 Risk 3 React Native for the patient app: one codebase for iOS and Android and matches the team's skills.
Trade-oﬀ: some native features take longer.
Use Epic's FHIR APIs for appointment sync instead of a custom HL7 v2 interface: faster to build and
standard. Trade-oﬀ: rate limits, some fields not exposed, and Lakeside must approve the app
registration.
Epic sandbox credentials requested from Sam Porter (Lakeside IT) on Feb 24 — still not received at the
start of Week 1.
Epic access delay pushes the EHR sync (Sprint 5) and therefore the pilot date.
SMS reminders need patient consent and approved opt-out wording (US TCPA rules) from Lakeside's
compliance team.
Suncoast's eligibility API documentation is incomplete; response formats may diﬀer from the docs.
PHI CareBook touches Patient name, date of birth, phone, email, insurance member ID, visit reason, visit type, provider, intake
answers (symptoms, medications). Rule: no PHI in logs, screenshots, test data, or AI tools.
Numbers clients quote Lakeside no-show rate 18% at the 3 pilot clinics (target under 10%) · phone booking takes about 7
minutes (online target: 2) · telehealth claim denial rate 12% at Suncoast, mostly eligibility errors

8. Phase 1 — Foundation
DAYS 1–30 · WEEKS 1–4
Foundation
Build the core habits every client conversation depends on: speak in structure, write in structure, ask better
questions, own the follow-up, and use AI before and after every call. All mocks are with the AI client; writing
drills start using your real project in Week 2.
Week Theme 1 Baseline and structured speaking 2 Structured writing 3 Asking better questions 4 Ownership and AI before/after
meetings
By Friday you can…
Open a call with a warm start and an agenda, introduce yourself in 30 seconds,
and answer any status question answer-first in under 45 seconds.
Write a status update, a recap email and a request that a busy American reader
understands in 10 seconds.
Run a 15-minute discovery conversation on a vague request, ask at least 8 useful
questions, and play the requirement back in 3 bullets.
Prepare for any call in 10 minutes with AI, send a recap within 2 hours, use owner
language, and handle "I don't know" calmly.

=== PAGE 24 ===
Week 1 — Baseline and structured speaking
PHASE 1 · FOUNDATION · WEEK 1 OF 13
By Friday you can open a call with a warm start and an agenda, introduce
yourself in 30 seconds, and answer any status question answer-first in under
45 seconds.
Pillars: Communicate
Why this matters on a client project. US clients decide in the first 30 seconds of a call whether you are clear and
in control. Answering first and speaking in a simple structure is the single habit that most changes how you are
perceived.
Key frameworks
Framework How it works
BLUF — Bottom Line Up
Front
Say the answer first, then the reason.
more detail, they will ask.
"Short answer: yes, Friday works. Here's why…
" If they want
PREP — Point, Reason,
Example, Point
For any explanation: "I recommend X (point), because Y (reason). For example, Z (example). So I'd
go with X (point).
"
The call opener (3 steps) 1. Warm start — 20–30 seconds of small talk: "How's your week going?"
,
"Is it getting cold in
Chicago yet?"
,
"How did the launch go?"
2. Purpose and agenda —
"The goal today is X. I have three things: A, B and C. Anything you'd like
to add?"
3. Time check —
"We have 30 minutes; I'll keep us on time.
"
The 30-second intro Who I am → what I own on this project → how I'll help you.
"Hi, I'm
___, the lead engineer on your
patient app. I own the build and the weekly updates, so I'm your main point of contact for anything
technical.
"

Card S01 — paste this into the AI as text, then switch to voice mode and speak first
You are role-playing a US client on a video call with me. Stay in character until I say "End the
call.
"
WHO YOU ARE: Dana Brooks, VP of Product at Brightpath Health (Austin, Texas). Fast, busy, outcome-
driven. Friendly but testing whether this new contact can be trusted.
PROJECT CONTEXT: My company is the engineering partner of Brightpath Health, a US telehealth
company. We are building CareBook v2 — patient booking (in-person and video), SMS reminders, intake
forms and a clinic schedule view — synced with the clinic's Epic EHR, with insurance eligibility
checks through Suncoast Billing. The launch customer is Lakeside Health (12 primary-care clinics in
Ohio). Everything is made up; no real patient data.
WHERE WE ARE NOW: Monday, March 2. Until now the delivery manager ran your calls. Today you meet the
lead engineer who is becoming your main contact. The pilot at 3 Lakeside clinics is planned for
April 27.
TODAY'S SCENARIO: Wait for me to open the call. If I don't set an agenda in the first minute, say
"So what's the plan for today?" Ask about the plan and how we'll keep you updated.
HIDDEN FACTS — reveal each one only if my questions lead to it:
1. Your previous vendor only sent updates when you asked, and you hated chasing them.
2. You prefer one written update every Friday before noon Central — short, with bullets.
3. Your biggest worry is the Epic integration; you've heard EHR integrations always run late.
4. You want one person you can text when something is urgent.
CURVEBALLS — use these during the call:
- Ask: "What will you do differently from the last team?"
- Ask something I probably can't answer: "How many pilots like this have you done in US healthcare?"
DIFFICULTY: Level 1 — friendly and patient; answer my questions fully.
HOW TO BEHAVE
- Speak like a real American business person: natural speed, short sentences, everyday expressions.
- Keep each turn short (1–3 sentences), like a real call.
- Don't volunteer information; give details only when I ask good questions.
- If I use technical jargon, ask me what it means for your business.
- If I talk for more than 45 seconds without getting to the point, cut in at my next pause.
WHEN I SAY "End the call"
, step out of character and give me feedback:
1. Score me 1–5 on each criterion that came up: opening and agenda, answer-first structure,
questions asked, playback, business link, trade-offs, handling unknowns, risks and bad news,
disagreement, closing and next steps.
2. List the hidden facts and tell me which ones I uncovered.
3. Quote 2 things I said well, and 2 things I should have said differently — with the better
sentence.
4. Tell me roughly how often I used filler words ("um"
,
"basically"
,
"actually"
,
"like").
5. Give me ONE thing to focus on in my next call.

MOCK CARD S03 · WEEK 1 · WEDNESDAY
Explain why the team chose a technology (React Native for the patient app)
Kevin Walsh (P4), CTO, Brightpath · Level 1 · 10–15 min · Skill: PREP structure
The story so far. It's Wednesday, March 4. Kevin missed the Sprint 1 design review in January, when the team
chose React Native for the patient app. A board member asked Kevin about app quality, so Kevin wants to hear the
reasoning directly from you.
Your fact sheet The decision Reasons Proof so far Trade-oﬀs Mitigation What you know before the call
Sprint 1 (January): build the patient app in React Native — one codebase for iOS and Android — instead
of two native apps.
1) One codebase: about 30% less build and test eﬀort than two native apps. 2) Skills: 3 of our 4 engineers
work in React daily. 3) Shared code with the React web portal (forms, validation).
App starts in 1.8 seconds on a mid-range Android phone. Booking flow works the same on iOS and
Android. 94 test cases pass on both.
Some native features take longer: the video-visit SDK needs a small native module (2 extra days, already
planned). Big OS updates need a library update within 2–4 weeks.
Pinned library versions, monthly dependency review, and a device test list of 12 phones, including older
Android models that Lakeside patients use.
Your goal: Explain the decision with PREP (point, reason, example, point) in under 90 seconds, and answer follow-
up questions without getting defensive.
What the client may throw at you: "Why not two native apps?"
Native breaks?"
·
"Why not Flutter?"
·
"What if Apple changes something and React
Step Example script — use it the first time, then try without it
Open
"Thanks for asking, Kevin — happy to walk you through it. Short version first, then any details you want.
"
Point"We chose React Native because it gets a reliable app to both iOS and Android faster, with one team.
"
Reason
"One codebase means about 30% less build and test eﬀort, and three of our four engineers work in React
every day.
"
Example
"For example, the booking flow we built in Sprint 2 works the same on both platforms, and the app starts in
under two seconds on a mid-range Android phone.
"
Trade-oﬀ"The honest trade-oﬀ: native features like the video SDK take a bit longer — we've planned two extra days for
that.
"
Point + close
"So React Native is still the right call for the pilot. I can send you a one-page decision record by Friday. Would
that help?"
What good looks like: ✓ Gave the point in the first sentence ✓ Reason and example came from real facts ✓ Named
the trade-oﬀ honestly ✓ Oﬀered a written follow-up with a date

=== PAGE 34 ===
Week 2 — Structured writing
PHASE 1 · FOUNDATION · WEEK 2 OF 13
By Friday you can write a status update, a recap email and a request that a
busy American reader understands in 10 seconds.
Pillars: Communicate · Own
Why this matters on a client project. With US clients, most communication is written because of the time
diﬀerence. Your messages are read while you are asleep. If they are unclear, the client loses a full day waiting for
your answer.
Key frameworks
Framework How it works
BLUF email The subject states the purpose and the ask ("ACTION: approve the login screen by Thu"). The first
two lines give the bottom line. Details follow in bullets. Everything fits on one screen.
Daily status (5 lines) Done · Next · Blocked · Risk · Need from you. One line each, and "none" is a valid answer.
The Pyramid Principle
(Barbara Minto)
Start with the main message. Group the supporting points under it (three at most). Back each point
with facts. The reader should be able to stop after the first line and still know what matters.
Plain US business English Short sentences, active voice, specific asks. Phrases that can confuse American readers: "Please do
the needful"
have a doubt"
"please"
, once.
→ name the exact action ·
"revert back"
→
"reply"
·
"prepone"
→
"move earlier"
·
"I
→
"I have a question"
·
"today itself"
→
"today"
·
"kindly" in every sentence →
Recap email Summary (2 lines) · Decisions · Action items (owner + date) · Open questions · Next meeting.
Template: Appendix A3.

The client asks for "a quick change" during a call
Dana Brooks (P1), VP of Product, Brightpath · Level 1 · 10–15 min · Skill: Confirm scope, recap
The story so far. It's Monday, March 9 — the last week of Sprint 3. During a quick check-in, Dana asks for an "Add
to calendar" button on the booking confirmation screen: "It's just a button — can you squeeze it in this week?"
Your fact sheet Sprint 3 left The ask Your estimate Options What you know before the call
Ends Fri Mar 13. Remaining: video booking (last 25%) · reschedule/cancel (3 days) · SMS reminders
(waiting on SMS number verification). The team is fully booked this week.
An "Add to calendar" button after booking. It needs calendar files for Apple, Google and Outlook, and
must work in the app and on the web.
1.5–2 days of build + half a day of testing. Adding it this week would push reschedule/cancel past the
Friday demo.
A) Add it to Sprint 4 (starts Mon Mar 16), ready for the Mar 27 demo. B) Swap it in this week and move
reschedule/cancel to Sprint 4.
Your recommendation Option A: keep Sprint 3 as planned and deliver the button in Sprint 4.
Your goal: Don't say yes or no on the spot. Clarify exactly what the change is, explain the impact on this sprint,
agree an option, and confirm it in writing.
What the client may throw at you: "It's just a button, right?"
"Why can't you do both this week?"
·
"Can it also add the clinic address and a map link?"
Step Example script — use it the first time, then try without it
Acknowledge
"That's a great idea — it should help patients remember visits. Can I ask two quick questions so I get it right?"
Clarify"Which calendars matter most — Apple and Google? And should the entry include the clinic address or the
video link?"
Impact"It's about two days of work. If we add it this week, reschedule and cancel would slip past Friday's demo.
"
Options
"We can add it to Sprint 4 and show it on March 27, or swap it in now and move reschedule and cancel. I'd
recommend Sprint 4.
"
Playback"So: an Add-to-calendar button for Apple and Google, with the address and video link, in Sprint 4. Did I get that
right?"
Close
"I'll confirm this in writing today so we're both clear.
"
What good looks like: ✓ Asked what exactly before saying yes or no ✓ Explained the impact on this sprint ✓ Gave
options with a recommendation ✓ Played back the agreed scope and promised a written recap

Card S05 — paste this into the AI as text, then switch to voice mode and speak first
You are role-playing a US client on a video call with me. Stay in character until I say "End the
call.
"
WHO YOU ARE: Dana Brooks, VP of Product at Brightpath Health (Austin, Texas). Busy, outcome-driven,
used to getting quick yeses.
PROJECT CONTEXT: My company is the engineering partner of Brightpath Health, a US telehealth
company. We are building CareBook v2 — patient booking (in-person and video), SMS reminders, intake
forms and a clinic schedule view — synced with the clinic's Epic EHR, with insurance eligibility
checks through Suncoast Billing. The launch customer is Lakeside Health (12 primary-care clinics in
Ohio). Everything is made up; no real patient data.
WHERE WE ARE NOW: Monday, March 9. Sprint 3 ends Friday. You just saw a competitor's app with an
"Add to calendar" button and want it in CareBook.
TODAY'S SCENARIO: Open with "Quick one — can we get an Add-to-calendar button after booking? It's
just a button. Can you squeeze it in this week?" If I agree immediately without asking anything, add
two more small requests.
HIDDEN FACTS — reveal each one only if my questions lead to it:
1. You only care about Apple and Google calendars.
2. You want the calendar entry to include the video link for video visits.
3. It's fine in Sprint 4 as long as it's ready for the investor demo on May 12.
CURVEBALLS — use these during the call:
- Ask: "Can it also add the clinic address and a map link?"
- Push once: "Come on, two days — can't someone work late?"
DIFFICULTY: Level 1 — friendly and patient; answer my questions fully.
HOW TO BEHAVE
- Speak like a real American business person: natural speed, short sentences, everyday expressions.
- Keep each turn short (1–3 sentences), like a real call.
- Don't volunteer information; give details only when I ask good questions.
- If I use technical jargon, ask me what it means for your business.
- If I talk for more than 45 seconds without getting to the point, cut in at my next pause.
WHEN I SAY "End the call"
, step out of character and give me feedback:
1. Score me 1–5 on each criterion that came up: opening and agenda, answer-first structure,
questions asked, playback, business link, trade-offs, handling unknowns, risks and bad news,
disagreement, closing and next steps.
2. List the hidden facts and tell me which ones I uncovered.
3. Quote 2 things I said well, and 2 things I should have said differently — with the better
sentence.
4. Tell me roughly how often I used filler words ("um"
,
"basically"
,
"actually"
,
"like").
5. Give me ONE thing to focus on in my next call.
Real meeting like this? Before it, run the master prep prompt (Appendix B6) with Meeting type = "change
request". After it, run the master meeting-notes prompt (Appendix B7).

Reminders will be live in staging 2 days late.

No impact on the April 27 pilot. Friday's demo will show reminders using test numbers either way.

reminder test to Dana's own phone.

MOCK CARD S33 · WEEK 2 · WEDNESDAY
Explain a bug to a non-technical client in pyramid form
Dr. Alan Mercer (P2), CMIO, Lakeside Health · Level 1 · 10–15 min · Skill: Pyramid: main message first
The story so far. It's Wednesday, March 11. On Monday, our QA engineer found a bug in staging: two patients
could book the same appointment slot at the same moment. Dr. Mercer heard about it from Dana and wants to
understand what it means for Lakeside's clinics.
Your fact sheet What happened Where The fix Impact What you know before the call
In testing, when two patients book the same slot within the same second, both could get a confirmation
— a double booking. It happened 3 times in 400 test bookings.
Staging (test system) only. No real patients — CareBook isn't live at Lakeside yet.
The system will "hold" a slot for a few seconds while one patient books it, so a second patient can't take
it. The tech lead is fixing it by Thu Mar 12; QA retests on Fri Mar 13.
None on the April 27 pilot. We're also adding an automatic test that tries double bookings in every
release.
Main message
"We found a double-booking bug in testing and are fixing it this week, before any patient could be
aﬀected.
"
Your goal: Explain the bug in pyramid form — main message first, then 3 supporting points — in plain words, with
no technical jargon.
What the client may throw at you: "Could this happen after go-live?"
do I explain this to my clinic managers?"
·
"What else haven't you found yet?"
·
"How
Step Example script — use it the first time, then try without it
Main message
"Here's the short version: we found a double-booking bug in testing, and we're fixing it this week — before any
patient could be aﬀected.
"
Point 1"What happened: if two patients tried to book the same slot at the same moment, both could get a
confirmation.
"
Point 2"The fix: the system will hold the slot for a few seconds while one person books it. That's done Thursday and
retested Friday.
"
Point 3"To stop it coming back, we're adding an automatic check that tests double bookings in every release.
"
Check"Does that answer your question, or would it help if I wrote two lines you can share with your clinic managers?"
What good looks like: ✓ Main message in the first sentence ✓ No jargon — explained in patient terms ✓ Three
clear supporting points ✓ Oﬀered something written to share

=== PAGE 42 ===
Week 3 — Asking better questions
PHASE 1 · FOUNDATION · WEEK 3 OF 13
By Friday you can run a 15-minute discovery conversation on a vague request,
ask at least 8 useful questions, and play the requirement back in 3 bullets.
Pillars: Understand
Why this matters on a client project. Clients rarely give complete requirements. The engineer who asks the right
questions early prevents rework, protects the timeline, and earns trust. Asking questions is also how you lead a
conversation instead of waiting to be asked.
Key frameworks
Framework How it works
Open vs closed questions Open questions explore: "What happens today when a patient misses an appointment?" Closed
questions confirm: "So reminders go out 24 hours before — correct?" Explore first, confirm last.
5 Whys (the polite version) Keep asking until you reach the business goal: "What's driving that?"
achieve?"
→
"How do you measure that today?"
→
"What would that help you
The question funnel Open → Probe ("Tell me more about…
"
,
"For example?") → Confirm ("So the must-have is…
").
Playback"Let me play that back to make sure I've got it: … Did I miss anything?" Use it after every important
point — not only at the end.
Discovery checklist Who uses it? What problem exists today? Why now? How is it done today? When is it needed?
How will we know it worked? (the success metric)
The Mom Test rule Ask about what people actually do and did (past behaviour), not what they might want.
this last happen?" beats "Would you use…?"
"When did

what's out of scope.

=== PAGE 54 ===
Week 4 — Ownership and AI before/after meetings
PHASE 1 · FOUNDATION · WEEK 4 OF 13
By Friday you can prepare for any call in 10 minutes with AI, send a recap
within 2 hours, use owner language, and handle "I don't know" calmly.
Pillars: Own · Understand · Stand firm
Why this matters on a client project. Without a PM, you are the owner. Clients trust engineers who say "I'll handle
it" and then close the loop. AI can make you much faster at preparing and following up — if you use it well and
safely.
Key frameworks
Framework How it works
Owner language vs waiting
language
"Should I…?"
→
"I'll…, unless you'd prefer otherwise.
"
"As you say.
"
→
"My recommendation is…
"
"I was waiting for…
"
→
"I followed up with… and here's the next step.
"
"That's not my area.
"
→
"I'll find out who owns that and get back to you by…
"
The follow-up loop Commit (say what and by when) → Confirm (write it in the recap) → Close (tell them when it's
done, by the date you gave).
AI before the call (10 min) Run the pre-call prep prompt (Appendix B3) → agenda, likely questions, risks, opener and closer →
edit it into your Pre-call card. Never go into a call cold.
AI after the call (10 min) Notes or transcript (no PHI) → post-call recap prompt (Appendix B4) → check every fact and
commitment yourself → send within 2 hours. You own the words, not the AI.
"I don't know — I'll check"
(5 steps)
1. Acknowledge: "Good question.
" 2. Be honest: "I don't have that detail right now.
" 3. Share what
you know: "What I can tell you is…
" 4. Commit: "I'll confirm by 10 am your time tomorrow.
" 5.
Follow up in writing. One short sentence of apology at most — usually none is needed.

you tend to use.

- Don't volunteer information; give details only when I ask good questions.
- If I use technical jargon, ask me what it means for your business.
- If I talk for more than 45 seconds without getting to the point, cut in at my next pause.

9. Phase 2 — Client craft
DAYS 31–60 · WEEKS 5–8
Client craft
Handle the real situations of client work: discovering requirements, managing expectations and trade-oﬀs,
disagreeing, delivering bad news, escalating, and keeping a US client informed asynchronously. Each week
also adds one area of US healthcare language: basics and HIPAA, EHR and FHIR, patient apps and telehealth,
and billing.

101

trade-oﬀs + EHR/interoperability

Billing/RCM
By Friday you can…
Explain in 2 minutes how money and data flow in US healthcare, know the basic
HIPAA rules for engineers, and run a structured 20-minute discovery call.
Map stakeholders, give estimates as ranges, explain a trade-oﬀ with a
recommendation in under 90 seconds, and handle a scope change with a written
change note.
Deliver bad news in the first 30 seconds with a plan, disagree professionally with
a senior client, and calm an upset client.
Escalate a blocker in one clear message, send an end-of-day update a client can
read in one minute, write a weekly status report, and explain the billing cycle in
simple words.

=== PAGE 65 ===
Week 5 — Requirement discovery + Healthcare 101
PHASE 2 · CLIENT CRAFT · WEEK 5 OF 13
By Friday you can explain in 2 minutes how money and data flow in US
healthcare, know the basic HIPAA rules for engineers, and run a structured 20-
minute discovery call.
Pillars: Understand · Domain: US healthcare basics, HIPAA
Why this matters on a client project. US healthcare clients expect you to understand their world — who pays, who
treats, what's regulated. You don't need to be an expert, but you must understand the business context behind a
feature.
Key frameworks
Framework How it works
US healthcare in one
picture
HIPAA for engineers (high
level)
Discovery call structure (7
steps)
User story + acceptance
criteria
Patients get care from providers (hospitals, clinics, doctors). Payers (insurance companies,
Medicare, Medicaid, employers) pay for most of it. Regulators (HHS, CMS, ONC) set the rules.
Vendors — EHR companies and health-tech companies like our clients — sell software to providers
and payers. Money flows from payers to providers; data flows between everyone.
PHI = health information that can identify a person (a name plus a diagnosis, date of birth, record
number…). Privacy Rule = who may see or share PHI. Security Rule = how to protect electronic
PHI (access control, encryption, audit logs). Minimum necessary = use only the PHI you need. BAA
= the contract that lets a vendor (often us) handle PHI for a client. Our rule: no PHI in logs, test data,
screenshots, or AI tools.
Context → Current state (how it works today) → Pain (what's broken, how often, what it costs) →
Desired outcome → Constraints (compliance, budget, systems, timeline) → Success metric →
Next steps.
"As a [user], I want [action] so that [outcome].
" Acceptance criteria: "Given… when… then…
Confirm them with the client, in writing.
"
.
Day by day — 45+ minutes a day

A value-based care provider group explains its business

The story so far. It's Monday, March 30, the first day of Sprint 5. Dana mentioned that Lakeside is "moving into
value-based contracts" and that it may change what they want from CareBook. Dr. Mercer has oﬀered 15 minutes to
explain how Lakeside's business works.
Your fact sheet What you know before the call
About Lakeside 12 primary-care clinics in Ohio, all on Epic. The CareBook pilot is at 3 of them (Westside, Northgate,
Riverbend), planned for Mon Apr 27.
Mostly fee-for-service: insurers (payers), Medicare and Medicaid pay Lakeside for each visit or service.

today
Value-based care What CareBook does Areas to explore Providers are paid partly for outcomes and quality, not just the number of visits (see the Week 5
framework box).
Online booking, SMS reminders, intake forms, clinic schedule view. No-show rate at the 3 pilot clinics:
18%.
Who pays them · which contract · what they are measured on · how much money is at stake · where
CareBook can help.
Your goal: Understand how Lakeside makes money under its new contract and which measures CareBook can
move, then play it back in 3 bullets.

Card S13 — paste this into the AI as text, then switch to voice mode and speak first
You are role-playing a US client on a video call with me. Stay in character until I say "End the
call.
"
WHO YOU ARE: Dr. Alan Mercer, Chief Medical Information Officer at Lakeside Health — 12 primary-care
clinics in Ohio that use Epic. Careful, clinical, happy to teach if the questions are good.
PROJECT CONTEXT: My company is the engineering partner of Brightpath Health, a US telehealth
company. We are building CareBook v2 — patient booking (in-person and video), SMS reminders, intake
forms and a clinic schedule view — synced with the clinic's Epic EHR, with insurance eligibility
checks through Suncoast Billing. The launch customer is Lakeside Health (12 primary-care clinics in
Ohio). Everything is made up; no real patient data.
WHERE WE ARE NOW: Monday, March 30. The CareBook pilot at 3 of your clinics is planned for April 27.
Lakeside is about to sign its first value-based contract.
TODAY'S SCENARIO: Open with "Dana said you wanted to understand our business. Where should I start?"
Keep answers general unless I ask specific questions. If I talk about technology instead of your
business, say "That's the how. I'm asking whether you understand the why.
"
HIDDEN FACTS — reveal each one only if my questions lead to it:
1. From July 1, Lakeside joins a value-based contract with a regional health insurer, covering about
9,000 of its patients.
2. Lakeside will be measured on: annual wellness visits completed, diabetes follow-up visits within
90 days, and avoidable ER visits.
3. If the targets are met, Lakeside can earn up to $1.2 million a year in shared savings; from year
3, missing them can cost money.
4. No-shows are 18% overall at the pilot clinics, but about 24% for diabetes follow-up visits —
which directly hurts the new measures.
5. You would love CareBook to remind patients who are overdue for a visit, not only patients who
already booked one.
CURVEBALLS — use these during the call:
- Ask: "Do you actually know what fee-for-service means?"
- Ask: "Why should an engineer care how we get paid?"
DIFFICULTY: Level 1 — friendly and patient; answer my questions fully.
HOW TO BEHAVE
- Speak like a real American business person: natural speed, short sentences, everyday expressions.
- Keep each turn short (1–3 sentences), like a real call.
- Don't volunteer information; give details only when I ask good questions.
- If I use technical jargon, ask me what it means for your business.
- If I talk for more than 45 seconds without getting to the point, cut in at my next pause.
WHEN I SAY "End the call"
, step out of character and give me feedback:
1. Score me 1–5 on each criterion that came up: opening and agenda, answer-first structure,
questions asked, playback, business link, trade-offs, handling unknowns, risks and bad news,
disagreement, closing and next steps.
2. List the hidden facts and tell me which ones I uncovered.
3. Quote 2 things I said well, and 2 things I should have said differently — with the better
sentence.
4. Tell me roughly how often I used filler words ("um"
,
"basically"
,
"actually"
,
"like").
5. Give me ONE thing to focus on in my next call.
Real meeting like this? Before it, run the master prep prompt (Appendix B6) with Meeting type = "discovery"
After it, run the master meeting-notes prompt (Appendix B7).

a high level
The story so far. It's Tuesday, March 31. Pacific Coast Health, a hospital network in California, is evaluating
CareBook, and Laura sent a security questionnaire last week. Laura has asked for 15 minutes on one question only:
how CareBook handles PHI in logs.
Your fact sheet Our rule How we enforce it Access and
encryption
Audit logging What you know before the call
No PHI in application logs. Logs use internal IDs — never names, date of birth, phone numbers, emails, or
visit reasons.
An automatic filter masks phone numbers and emails in logs.
code review. Test data is synthetic.
"No PHI in logs" is a checklist item in every
Data is encrypted in transit and at rest on AWS. Logs are in AWS with access limited to 3 named roles.
Brightpath has a BAA with AWS.
Being built in Sprint 5, due Fri Apr 10: records who viewed or changed an appointment or intake form,
when, and from where. Kept separate from app logs.
What you don't know The exact log retention period, and whether a BAA covers the crash-reporting tool. Kevin Walsh owns
both answers.
Your goal: Answer clearly at a high level, don't guess on anything you don't know, and commit to written follow-ups
with dates.
What the client may throw at you: "Do any third-party tools see PHI?"
exactly can access them?"
·
"How long do you keep logs?"
·

Open
"Hi Laura, thanks for the time. I understand today is about how CareBook handles PHI in logs. I'll give you the
short version first, then we can go deeper.
"
Answer
"Short answer: our application logs don't contain PHI. We log internal IDs, not names, phone numbers, or visit
"
reasons.
How
"Phone numbers and emails are masked automatically, and it's checked in every code review. Logs are

"
Audit"Separately, this sprint we're building an audit log — who viewed or changed a patient's appointment or intake
form, and when. It's due April 10.
"
Unknown

"
Close
"I'll send a short written summary today, and the retention answer by Thursday.

"
WHO YOU ARE: Laura Bennett, Compliance and Privacy Officer at Pacific Coast Health, a hospital

company. We are building CareBook v2 — patient booking (in-person and video), SMS reminders, intake
forms and a clinic schedule view — synced with the clinic's Epic EHR, with insurance eligibility
checks through Suncoast Billing. The launch customer is Lakeside Health (12 primary-care clinics in
Ohio). Everything is made up; no real patient data.
WHERE WE ARE NOW: Tuesday, March 31. You sent the CareBook team a security questionnaire last week.
Today you want to check whether they really understand PHI.

your logs?" If I give a vague answer like "we follow best practices"

HIDDEN FACTS — reveal each one only if my questions lead to it:
1. Last year, another vendor exposed patient phone numbers through debug logs. That is why you are
strict about logging.
2. Your internal policy requires audit logs to be kept for 6 years.
3. You need written answers before your review committee meets on April 9.
4. If the logging answers are good, it is the last open item before Pacific Coast Health moves to
contract talks.
CURVEBALLS — use these during the call:
- Ask: "Does your crash-reporting tool ever capture screenshots with patient data on them?"
- Ask: "Would your company sign a BAA with us directly?"

WHO YOU ARE: Dana Brooks, VP of Product at Brightpath Health (Austin, Texas). Fast, busy, outcome-
driven.
PROJECT CONTEXT: My company is the engineering partner of Brightpath Health, a US telehealth
company. We are building CareBook v2 — patient booking (in-person and video), SMS reminders, intake
forms and a clinic schedule view — synced with the clinic's Epic EHR, with insurance eligibility
checks through Suncoast Billing. The launch customer is Lakeside Health (12 primary-care clinics in
Ohio). Everything is made up; no real patient data.
WHERE WE ARE NOW: Wednesday, April 1. Sprint 5 ends April 10, and the pilot at 3 Lakeside clinics is
planned for April 27. You want new patients to book video visits online.

even know what I need yet.
"
HIDDEN FACTS — reveal each one only if my questions lead to it:
1. The 3 pilot clinics get about 420 new-patient requests a month; about 1 in 4 hang up before they
manage to book.
2. New patients must choose a provider who is accepting new patients, and give insurance details
before the visit so they can be checked.
3. Patients under 18 need a parent or guardian to book; Lakeside wants them excluded at first.
4. Success means 40% of new-patient bookings happen online within 3 months of launch.
5. You would accept this after the pilot (in Sprint 7) if the pilot date stays safe — but only say
so if I ask what matters more.
CURVEBALLS — use these during the call:
- Ask: "Can't you just reuse the existing booking screen?"
- Ask: "Ballpark — two days?"
DIFFICULTY: Level 2 — busy and vague; short answers, change topic, slightly impatient.

MOCK CARD S37 · WEEK 5 · THURSDAY
Confirm acceptance criteria with the client
Dana Brooks (P1), VP of Product, Brightpath · Level 2 · 10–15 min · Skill: User stories and acceptance criteria
The story so far. It's Thursday, April 2. After yesterday's discovery, Dana agreed that new-patient video booking will
be built in Sprint 7 (May 11–22), after the pilot. You drafted the first user story and 4 acceptance criteria. Dana has
10 minutes to confirm them.
Your fact sheet What you know before the call
User story"As a new patient, I want to book a video visit online so that I don't have to wait on the phone.
"
AC 1 AC 2 AC 3 and AC 4 Open questions Timing Given a new patient, when they choose a provider, then only providers accepting new patients are shown.
Given the booking form, when the patient submits, then insurance member ID and date of birth are
required before the visit is confirmed.
Patients under 18 are asked to call the clinic (phase 1). Every booked visit gets an SMS and email
confirmation within 1 minute.
1) If insurance can't be verified — book anyway, or block? 2) Who approves the new patient in Epic, and
how fast?
Planned for Sprint 7 (May 11–22). Anything new today goes to a later sprint.
Your goal: Get a clear yes or no on each acceptance criterion, close the 2 open questions, and confirm everything
in writing.
What the client may throw at you: "Can we add one more thing?"
"Why can't this go into the pilot?"
·
"What happens if the insurance check fails?"
Step Example script — use it the first time, then try without it
Open
"Hi Dana, this is a quick one. I'd like your yes or no on four acceptance criteria for new-patient video booking,
and answers to two open questions.
"
Walk through"First: only providers accepting new patients show up. Yes?"
Check"Second: insurance member ID and date of birth are required before we confirm the visit. Is that right for every
new patient?"
Open question
"If insurance can't be verified, should we still book the visit and flag it for the front desk, or block the booking?"
Scope
"Anything new today goes on the list for Sprint 8, so Sprint 7 stays on track. Is that okay?"
Close
"I'll send the final story and criteria within the hour. If I don't hear back by Friday noon your time, I'll treat them
as approved.
"
·
What good looks like: ✓ A clear yes or no on each criterion ✓ Captured the self-pay and "flag, don't block"
answers ✓ Parked new asks for a later sprint ✓ Written confirmation with an "approved unless corrected by" date

Client-Ready Engineer · 90-Day Upskilling Plan Page 76 of 198

=== PAGE 77 ===
Week 6 — Stakeholders, expectations and trade-oﬀs + EHR/interoperability
PHASE 2 · CLIENT CRAFT · WEEK 6 OF 13
By Friday you can map stakeholders, give estimates as ranges, explain a
trade-oﬀ with a recommendation in under 90 seconds, and handle a scope
change with a written change note.
Pillars: Communicate · Own · Domain: EHR, HL7, FHIR
Why this matters on a client project. Most client friction comes from mismatched expectations about dates, scope
or quality. Setting expectations clearly — and explaining trade-oﬀs in business terms — prevents conflicts before
they start.
Key frameworks
Framework How it works
Stakeholder map For each person: role · what they care about · how much influence they have · how often to update
them. For example: CTO (stability, security) vs VP of Product (speed, features) vs end users (ease of
use).
Estimates as ranges +
confidence
"3 to 5 days, and I'm fairly confident. The unknown is the EHR sandbox — I'll know more by
Wednesday.
" Never give a single number for uncertain work.
The trade-oﬀ formula "We have two options. Option A gives [benefit] but [cost]. Option B gives [benefit] but [cost]. I
recommend [X] because [business outcome]. Are you comfortable with that?"
Scope, time and quality If scope grows, time or quality must change. Say it early: "We can add this. To keep the date, we'd
move X to the next sprint. Which do you prefer?"
Change note What changed · why · impact (time, cost, risk) · options · the decision needed and by when.
Template: Appendix A8.
EHR and interoperability, in
plain terms
EHR = the digital patient chart used by providers (Epic, Oracle Health/Cerner, athenahealth).
Interoperability = systems sharing data. HL7 v2 = an older message standard (e.g. an "ADT"
message when a patient is admitted). FHIR = the modern API standard; data comes as "resources"
(Patient, Appointment, Observation, Encounter). SMART on FHIR = how apps launch inside an EHR
with secure login.
Day by day — 45+ minutes a day
Day Prep · 5 min Learn · 10 min Mock · 10–15 min Write · 10 min

your current project. (Or
use CareBook's Who's
who, section 7.5.)

diﬀerent things (the AI plays both

A stakeholder map table for
your real project (or for
CareBook).

estimate ranges and the
unknowns.
Estimates as ranges. S18 "Can you do it by Friday?"
(P1, Level 2).
An estimate message:
range, confidence, and
what could change it.

"

.
PROJECT CONTEXT: My company is the engineering partner of Brightpath Health, a US telehealth
company. We are building CareBook v2 — patient booking (in-person and video), SMS reminders, intake
forms and a clinic schedule view — synced with the clinic's Epic EHR, with insurance eligibility
checks through Suncoast Billing. The launch customer is Lakeside Health (12 primary-care clinics in
Ohio). Everything is made up; no real patient data.
WHERE WE ARE NOW: Monday, April 6. Epic sandbox access finally arrived this morning, six weeks late.
The pilot at 3 Lakeside clinics is planned for April 27.
TODAY'S SCENARIO: DANA opens: "I need the waitlist in the pilot.
" KEVIN replies: "And I need a code
freeze on April 17.
" Then both wait for me. If I agree with whoever spoke last, the other one says
"So you're just going to say yes to everyone?"
HIDDEN FACTS — reveal each one only if my questions lead to it:

2. DANA: Lakeside never asked for the waitlist in the pilot.
3. KEVIN: A rushed release last year caused a 3-hour outage at a clinic, and the CEO still brings it
up.

environment — if I suggest it.
5. BOTH: Dana owns the scope decision; Kevin owns the release decision.
CURVEBALLS — use these during the call:
- DANA asks: "Can the team work over the weekend to do both?"
- KEVIN asks: "Whose side are you on here?"
DIFFICULTY: Level 2 — busy and vague; short answers, change topic, slightly impatient.
HOW TO BEHAVE
- Speak like a real American business person: natural speed, short sentences, everyday expressions.
- Keep each turn short (1–3 sentences), like a real call.
- Don't volunteer information; give details only when I ask good questions.
- If I use technical jargon, ask me what it means for your business.
- If I talk for more than 45 seconds without getting to the point, cut in at my next pause.
WHEN I SAY "End the call"
, step out of character and give me feedback:
1. Score me 1–5 on each criterion that came up: opening and agenda, answer-first structure,
questions asked, playback, business link, trade-offs, handling unknowns, risks and bad news,
disagreement, closing and next steps.
2. List the hidden facts and tell me which ones I uncovered.
3. Quote 2 things I said well, and 2 things I should have said differently — with the better
sentence.
4. Tell me roughly how often I used filler words ("um"
,
"basically"
,
"actually"
,
"like").
5. Give me ONE thing to focus on in my next call.

project.

Lakeside enabled.
8–10 days. Standard, and Lakeside IT already approved the app. Cost: sync every 5 minutes, not
instantly; visit reason missing (patients enter it in CareBook).
20–25 days. Real-time and all fields, but more to maintain, and Lakeside's interface team has a 6-week

A for the pilot. Measure sync delays during the pilot and revisit HL7 before the summer rollout if needed.

You are role-playing a US client on a video call with me. Stay in character until I say "End the

"
WHO YOU ARE: Dana Brooks, VP of Product at Brightpath Health (Austin, Texas). Fast, persuasive,
under pressure from the CEO.
PROJECT CONTEXT: My company is the engineering partner of Brightpath Health, a US telehealth
company. We are building CareBook v2 — patient booking (in-person and video), SMS reminders, intake
forms and a clinic schedule view — synced with the clinic's Epic EHR, with insurance eligibility
checks through Suncoast Billing. The launch customer is Lakeside Health (12 primary-care clinics in
Ohio). Everything is made up; no real patient data.
WHERE WE ARE NOW: Friday, April 10, the last day of Sprint 5. The pilot at 3 Lakeside clinics is on
April 27. This week you saw a competitor's app with insurance card photo upload.
TODAY'S SCENARIO: Open with "Tiny request — I need insurance card photo upload in the intake form
for the pilot. Can you just add it?" Push back hard and insist it's small. If I say yes without
naming the trade-off, add another request: "Great — and a pharmacy picker too.
" If I say a flat no,
say "That's not the partner attitude I expected.
"
HIDDEN FACTS — reveal each one only if my questions lead to it:
1. A competitor showed insurance card upload at a conference this week, and your CEO asked about it.
2. What you really need is to show it in the investor demo on May 12 — not to have it live for pilot
patients.
3. Lakeside's front desk (Lisa Chen) actually prefers typed member IDs, because they are easier to
check in Epic.
4. You would accept "demo-ready in a test environment by May 8" if it is offered.
CURVEBALLS — use these during the call:
- Say: "Our competitor built it in a week — why can't you?"
- Ask: "Why does a photo need Kevin's security review?"
DIFFICULTY: Level 3 — sceptical; push back, interrupt at pauses, ask hard questions, apply time
pressure.
HOW TO BEHAVE
- Speak like a real American business person: natural speed, short sentences, everyday expressions.
- Keep each turn short (1–3 sentences), like a real call.
- Don't volunteer information; give details only when I ask good questions.
- If I use technical jargon, ask me what it means for your business.
- If I talk for more than 45 seconds without getting to the point, cut in at my next pause.
WHEN I SAY "End the call"
, step out of character and give me feedback:
1. Score me 1–5 on each criterion that came up: opening and agenda, answer-first structure,
questions asked, playback, business link, trade-offs, handling unknowns, risks and bad news,
disagreement, closing and next steps.
2. List the hidden facts and tell me which ones I uncovered.
3. Quote 2 things I said well, and 2 things I should have said differently — with the better
sentence.
4. Tell me roughly how often I used filler words ("um"
,
"basically"
,
"actually"
,
"like").
5. Give me ONE thing to focus on in my next call.

request". After it, run the master meeting-notes prompt (Appendix B7).

=== PAGE 89 ===
Week 7 — Conflict, disagreement and bad news
PHASE 2 · CLIENT CRAFT · WEEK 7 OF 13
By Friday you can deliver bad news in the first 30 seconds with a plan,
disagree professionally with a senior client, and calm an upset client.
Pillars: Stand firm · Own · Domain: Patient apps and telehealth
Why this matters on a client project. Every project has bad news: delays, bugs, wrong assumptions. US clients
lose trust when they hear bad news late or indirectly — not when they hear it early with a plan. Respectful
disagreement is expected; silent agreement followed by a missed date is not.
Key frameworks
Framework How it works
The bad-news formula (6
steps)
1. Headline: "I have some bad news about the release date.
" 2. Impact: "The release moves from
March 12 to March 19.
" 3. Cause — short, no blame: "The pharmacy API returns data in a diﬀerent
format than documented.
" 4. Options: "We can release on time without refills, or release everything
a week later.
" 5. What I need: "I need your decision by Thursday.
" 6. Next update: "I'll update you
on Wednesday either way.
" One short apology is enough.
Disagree professionally Acknowledge their goal → state your concern with facts → oﬀer an alternative → ask.
"I understand
you want this live by Friday for the investor demo. My concern is that skipping testing risks wrong
appointment times for patients. Could we demo the core flow on Friday and release fully on
Tuesday?"
Listen (don't interrupt) → Acknowledge ("I understand this caused problems for your front desk")
→ Solve (what you've done, what's next) → Thank ("Thanks for flagging this so quickly").
"Not this sprint — but here's when.
"
·
"Yes, if we move X.
"
·
"Here's what I can do instead.
"
Handling an upset client
(LAST)
Saying no without saying
"no"
Pause phrases (to buy time
calmly)
Patient apps and
telehealth, in plain terms
"Let me think about that for a second.
"
·
"That's a fair point — let me make sure I understand.
"
"Can I come back to that once I've checked the numbers?"
·
Scheduling and no-shows — missed appointments cost clinics money. Reminders by SMS or
email need patient consent. Telehealth = video or phone visits. RPM (remote patient monitoring) =
devices that send readings to care teams. Patient portals = where patients see records, book visits
and send messages. Accessibility — apps must work for older users and people with disabilities
(WCAG).

Epic appointment sync: some times show 1 hour oﬀ, and some cancellations don't come through. The
tech lead is fixing it; estimate 3–4 days.
Wrong appointment times reaching patients and front desks, double bookings, and Lakeside losing trust
in the first week of the pilot.
Risk-based regression: 1.5 days on the 60 highest-risk cases (booking, Epic sync, reminders) plus
automated smoke tests. Keep UAT on Apr 22.
By Wed Apr 15 we will know if the Epic fix is done. That is when we can say whether Apr 27 still holds.
Agreement to keep testing on the critical flows, and a go/no-go check-in on Wednesday.

The release slips by one week
Dana Brooks (P1), VP of Product, Brightpath · Level 2 · 10–15 min · Skill: Bad-news formula
The story so far. It's Tuesday, April 14. Our tech lead has confirmed the Epic appointment sync problem is bigger
than expected: the fix plus retesting needs 5 working days. The pilot cannot safely go live on April 27. You need to

Your fact sheet What you know before the call
Headline Cause The fix Option A The pilot moves from Mon Apr 27 to Mon May 4.
Epic returns appointment times in a diﬀerent time-zone format, and uses diﬀerent status codes for
cancelled and no-show appointments than Lakeside's documentation says.
Tech lead's estimate: 3 days to fix the time and status mapping, plus 2 days to retest the sync with
Lakeside's Epic sandbox.
Go live Apr 27 without Epic sync: front-desk staﬀ re-enter every online booking into Epic by hand. Fast,
but double work and a high risk of errors.
Go live May 4 with full sync and full testing. The investor demo on May 12 is still safe.
Option B
(recommended)
Need from Dana A decision by Wed Apr 15, noon CT, and a joint call with Dr. Mercer on Thursday to tell Lakeside. Next
update: Thu Apr 16.
Your goal: Deliver the bad news in the first 30 seconds using the 6-step formula, with options, a recommendation,
and a clear next update time.

am I only hearing this now?"
·

·
"Why
Step Example script — use it the first time, then try without it
Headline
"Dana, I have some bad news about the pilot date. We need to move it from April 27 to May 4.
"
Cause
"Epic sends appointment times and cancellation codes in a diﬀerent format than the documentation. Our tech
lead needs three days to fix it and two days to retest.
"
Options
"We have two options. We go live on the 27th without Epic sync, and the front desks re-enter bookings by hand
— or we go live on May 4 with everything working.
"
Recommend"I recommend May 4. Manual re-entry is exactly how wrong appointment times happen. And the May 12
investor demo is still safe.
"
Ask"I need your decision by noon tomorrow, and I'd like to set up a call with Dr. Mercer on Thursday so we tell
Lakeside together.
"
Close
"I'll send this in writing right after the call, and I'll update you on Thursday either way.
"
What good looks like: ✓ Headline in the first 30 seconds ✓ Cause explained briefly with no blame ✓ Two options
and a clear recommendation ✓ Decision deadline and next update time given

=== PAGE 101 ===
Week 8 — Escalation and async updates + Billing/RCM
PHASE 2 · CLIENT CRAFT · WEEK 8 OF 13
By Friday you can escalate a blocker in one clear message, send an end-of-
day update a client can read in one minute, write a weekly status report, and
explain the billing cycle in simple words.
Pillars: Own · Communicate · Domain: Billing and revenue cycle (RCM)
Why this matters on a client project. Without a PM, nobody else chases blockers or keeps the client informed. US
clients are oﬄine for most of your working day, so written updates are how you show progress and ownership. Many
US healthcare projects also touch billing — the process that decides whether a clinic gets paid.
Key frameworks
Framework How it works
When to escalate The escalation message (5
parts)
Async updates for US time
zones
Weekly status report (RAG) Billing / RCM, in plain terms Escalate when a blocker lasts more than 1–2 days, a date is at risk, or a decision is outside your
authority. Escalate early, to the right person, in writing. Escalating is not complaining — it is
protecting the client's timeline.
What's blocked → Impact (date, cost, risk) → What I've tried → What I need → By when. For
example: "Quick flag: we're still waiting for access to the Epic test system, so the integration demo
moves from Mar 12 to Mar 19. I've followed up with your IT team twice. Could you help unblock it by
Wednesday? If not, I'll demo with mock data.
"
End-of-day (EOD) note, sent before you log oﬀ so it's waiting in their morning: Done today · Plan
for tomorrow · Questions for you, numbered so they can reply "1: yes, 2: next week"
. Use a 2-
minute Loom video when showing UI or explaining something visual.
Overall status: Green (on track) / Amber (at risk, plan in place) / Red (oﬀ track, need help), with one
line on why. Then: highlights · next week · risks and what we're doing about them · decisions
needed.
RCM (revenue cycle management) = how a clinic gets paid. 1. Patient books → 2. Eligibility check (is
the insurance active?) → 3. Visit → 4. Coding (ICD-10 = diagnosis, CPT = procedure) → 5. Claim
sent to the payer, often through a clearinghouse (electronic claim = X12 837) → 6. Payer pays or
denies (payment file = X12 835) → 7. Denials are fixed and resubmitted → 8. Patient pays the rest.
Metrics clients watch: denial rate, days in A/R, clean-claim rate. Prior authorization = payer
approval needed before some treatments.

=== PAGE 114 ===
Week 9 — Technical storytelling + first live shadowing
PHASE 3 · LIVE REPS · WEEK 9 OF 13
By Friday you can explain any piece of technical work at 3 levels (30 seconds, 2
minutes, 10 minutes), and you have shadowed 2 live client calls with notes.
Pillars: Communicate · Understand
Why this matters on a client project. Clients remember stories, not specifications. Explaining technical work as a
short story with a business result helps non-technical clients understand and value it. From this week you also start
learning from real client calls.
Key frameworks
Framework How it works
The technical story
(CPARB)
Context → Problem → Approach → Result → Business impact.
"The check-in page was slow
(context). Front-desk staﬀ waited 6 seconds per patient (problem). We cached the insurance lookups
(approach). It now takes under a second (result). That saves about 15 minutes of staﬀ time per clinic
per day (business impact).
"
Three levels Executive (30 seconds): result + impact. Manager (2 minutes): + approach + risks. Engineer (10
minutes): + technical detail. Start at the top level and go deeper only if they ask.
Analogies
"FHIR is like a standard plug shape — every system can connect to it.
" Use one analogy, then return
to the facts.
The "So what?" test After each sentence, ask: "So what does that mean for the client?" If there's no answer, cut the
sentence.
Shadowing Join as an observer: camera on, muted unless invited. Fill in the observation sheet (Appendix A9).
Afterwards, write your own recap and compare it with the lead's.
Day by day — 45+ minutes a day

work you did.

clinics, and about 3.4 hours a day once all 12 are live.

billing is still protected.

HIDDEN FACTS — reveal each one only if my questions lead to it:
1. Lisa Chen emailed you on April 16 saying the staff were "fighting the system" at check-in; you
still need to reply to Lisa.
2. You worry "saved insurance data" sounds like a privacy risk — but only raise it if I mention
caching.
3. Your leadership team cares about staff time saved, not seconds.
CURVEBALLS — use these during the call:
- Ask: "So was it broken before? Why didn't we catch it earlier?"
- Ask one question I probably can't answer: "Will it be as fast at the other 9 clinics with older
computers?"
DIFFICULTY: Level 2 — busy and vague; short answers, change topic, slightly impatient.
HOW TO BEHAVE
- Speak like a real American business person: natural speed, short sentences, everyday expressions.
- Keep each turn short (1–3 sentences), like a real call.
- Don't volunteer information; give details only when I ask good questions.
- If I use technical jargon, ask me what it means for your business.
- If I talk for more than 45 seconds without getting to the point, cut in at my next pause.
WHEN I SAY "End the call"
, step out of character and give me feedback:
1. Score me 1–5 on each criterion that came up: opening and agenda, answer-first structure,
questions asked, playback, business link, trade-offs, handling unknowns, risks and bad news,
disagreement, closing and next steps.
2. List the hidden facts and tell me which ones I uncovered.
3. Quote 2 things I said well, and 2 things I should have said differently — with the better
sentence.
4. Tell me roughly how often I used filler words ("um"
,
"basically"
,
"actually"
,
"like").
5. Give me ONE thing to focus on in my next call.

cleared on any insurance change. Encrypted at rest, same HIPAA-covered AWS account, no PHI in logs.

every 5 minutes.

About 1 day of work.
Your goal: Tell the same story twice — plain and outcome-first for Dr. Mercer, then precise and technical for Kevin
— without changing any fact.

Explain a FHIR integration to a non-technical clinic manager
Lisa Chen, Clinic Operations Manager, Lakeside (invited by Dr. Alan Mercer, P2) · Level 2 · 10–15 min · Skill:
Analogies, plain language
The story so far. It's Thursday, April 30, two working days before the pilot. Dr. Mercer has asked you to explain to
Lisa Chen how CareBook "talks to Epic"
, because Lisa briefs 14 front-desk staﬀ tomorrow morning.
Your fact sheet What it does Who is in charge How it works What was fixed What staﬀ see Support What you know before the call
When a patient books, reschedules or cancels in CareBook, the change appears in Epic within about 1
minute. Changes made in Epic flow back to CareBook too.
Epic stays the "source of truth" for each provider's schedule. CareBook only oﬀers slots that Epic shows
as free.
CareBook uses Epic's standard FHIR interface — an approved, secure connection into Epic — so nobody
re-types appointments.
In Week 7, Epic sent times and statuses in a diﬀerent format from the documentation. Fixed Apr 16; 500
test appointments since then, zero mismatches.
A slot marked "held" in CareBook means a patient is booking it right now; it frees itself after 5 minutes.
Staﬀ keep working in Epic as usual.
If something looks wrong, call Brightpath support (Jordan Lee's team), 7 am–7 pm ET on weekdays. Staﬀ
training was on Apr 28; a 30-minute refresher is on Friday May 1, 7:30 am ET.
Your goal: Explain the integration with one simple analogy and no jargon, so Lisa can repeat it to the staﬀ tomorrow.
What the client may throw at you: "So which one is the real schedule?"
"What happens if our internet goes down?"
·
"Could two patients get the same slot?"
·
Step Example script — use it the first time, then try without it
Open
"Hi Lisa, thanks for making time before tomorrow's training. I'll explain how CareBook and Epic work together,
then answer the questions your staﬀ will ask.
"
Analogy"Think of a shared calendar. Epic owns the calendar; CareBook is a second screen that reads and writes to it
through an approved connection.
"
In practice
"When a patient books online, it shows up in Epic within about a minute. If your staﬀ cancel in Epic, CareBook
sees that too.
"
Reassure
"After last week's fix we tested 500 appointments and found no mismatches.
"
Staﬀ tip"If staﬀ see a slot marked 'held'
, a patient is booking it right now. It frees up after 5 minutes.
"
Check"How would you explain this to your team in one sentence? I want to make sure it's simple enough.
"
Close
"I'll send you a one-page cheat sheet today, with the support number and hours.
"
What good looks like: ✓ One clear analogy, then back to facts ✓ No unexplained jargon ✓ Answered "which is the
real schedule" clearly ✓ Oﬀered a cheat sheet and support contact with hours

=== PAGE 122 ===
Week 10 — Demos + co-lead a live call
PHASE 3 · LIVE REPS · WEEK 10 OF 13
By Friday you can run a 10-minute demo as a user story, handle questions and
a demo failure calmly, and you have owned one agenda item on a live call.
Pillars: Communicate · Own
Why this matters on a client project. Demos are where clients see progress and give feedback. A structured demo
builds confidence in you and the team; a messy demo creates doubt even when the work is good.
Key frameworks
Framework How it works
Demo structure 1. Goal recap: "Last sprint we agreed to make booking faster for patients.
" 2. Show the result first
("do the last thing first") — the finished screen or outcome. 3. Walk the user journey as a story:
"Imagine Maria, a patient, booking a visit at 10 pm…
" 4. What's next + known gaps. 5. Ask for
specific feedback: "Does this flow match how your front desk works?"
Demo checklist Test data only — never real PHI · rehearse once end to end · backup video or screenshots ·
notifications oﬀ, other apps closed · timebox it (10 min demo, 10 min questions) · recap draft ready.
When the demo breaks Stay calm and name it ("Looks like the test server is slow"), switch to the backup, move on, and
follow up in the recap. Don't debug live for more than 30 seconds.
Collecting feedback Ask open questions, write down their exact words, play back the key feedback, and confirm what
goes into the next sprint.
Co-leading Agree beforehand with the PM or senior which agenda item is yours. Prepare it like a full call. They
step in only if you signal.
Day by day — 45+ minutes a day
Day Prep · 5 min Learn · 10 min Mock · 10–15 min Write · 10 min

2. You plan to show this waitlist to investors on May 12, so the freeze worries you.
3. As Lisa: staff want the patient's phone number shown right on the waitlist, without an extra
click.
CURVEBALLS — use these during the call:
- Ask: "If it froze now, will it freeze for my staff during the pilot?"
- Ask: "Can the waitlist text patients automatically?"
DIFFICULTY: Level 2 — busy and vague; short answers, change topic, slightly impatient.
HOW TO BEHAVE
- Speak like a real American business person: natural speed, short sentences, everyday expressions.
- Keep each turn short (1–3 sentences), like a real call.
- Don't volunteer information; give details only when I ask good questions.
- If I use technical jargon, ask me what it means for your business.
- If I talk for more than 45 seconds without getting to the point, cut in at my next pause.
WHEN I SAY "End the call"
, step out of character and give me feedback:
1. Score me 1–5 on each criterion that came up: opening and agenda, answer-first structure,
questions asked, playback, business link, trade-offs, handling unknowns, risks and bad news,
disagreement, closing and next steps.
2. List the hidden facts and tell me which ones I uncovered.
3. Quote 2 things I said well, and 2 things I should have said differently — with the better
sentence.
4. Tell me roughly how often I used filler words ("um"
,
"basically"
,
"actually"
,
"like").
5. Give me ONE thing to focus on in my next call.
