=== PAGE 130 ===
Week 11 — Product thinking + co-lead
PHASE 3 · LIVE REPS · WEEK 11 OF 13
By Friday you can link your project's features to the client's business metrics,
suggest one improvement unprompted, and co-lead the requirements part of a
live call.
Pillars: Understand · Own · Domain: Healthcare business metrics
Why this matters on a client project. The engineers clients value most don't just build what is asked. They
understand the goal and suggest better ways to reach it. This is what "speak as an owner" looks like at its best.
Key frameworks
Framework How it works
Product questions Who is the user? What problem do they have? How do we know it's a problem (data)? What does
success look like (metric)? What is the smallest version that proves it (MVP)?
Healthcare business
metrics
Providers: no-show rate, patient wait time, time-to-chart (how long doctors spend on
documentation), patient satisfaction. Billing: denial rate, days in A/R, clean-claim rate. Digital
health: activation, engagement, retention. Compliance: audit findings, access reviews completed.
Impact vs eﬀort Place ideas on a simple 2×2 grid. Do high-impact, low-eﬀort ideas first. Say it out loud to the client:
"This one is high impact and about two days of work.
"
Proactive suggestion
format
"I noticed [observation]. We could [idea]. It would help [metric or business outcome]. Eﬀort: [S / M /
L]. Want me to write it up?"
MVP thinking"What could we ship in 1–2 weeks to learn whether this works?"
Day by day — 45+ minutes a day

features.

MOCK CARD S31 · WEEK 11 · TUESDAY
Propose an improvement the client didn't ask for
Maria Lopez (P3), Director of Revenue Cycle, Suncoast Billing · Level 2 · 10–15 min · Skill: Product thinking
The story so far. It's Tuesday, May 12. Yesterday Maria explained that most telehealth denials are eligibility
problems found too late — at check-in. You have an idea nobody asked for: run the check 72 hours before each
visit, so problems are fixed before the patient arrives.
Your fact sheet The problem The cost Your idea Eﬀort Small first test Open questions What you know before the call
12% of telehealth claims are denied, and about 7 in 10 of those are eligibility issues. With about 900
claims a month, that's about 75 eligibility denials a month at the pilot clinics.
About 30 minutes of rework per denied claim (about 38 staﬀ hours a month), and around $6,750 a month
in delayed or lost payments (75 × $90).
Run the eligibility check automatically 72 hours before each visit. If it fails, text the patient to update their
insurance and flag it for the front desk.
Medium: about 6 working days. It reuses the existing Suncoast check and the SMS reminder flow.
Start with video visits at the 3 pilot clinics for 2 weeks, and compare denial rates with in-person visits.
Does Suncoast's contract limit how many checks CareBook can run? Does Lakeside's compliance team
need to approve the new text message?
Your goal: Propose the idea in a clear structure — what you noticed, the idea, the metric it moves, the eﬀort — and
agree a next step with Maria.
What the client may throw at you: "How do you know it will work?"
not just fix it at check-in?"
·
"Will this cost us more per check?"
·
"Why
Step Example script — use it the first time, then try without it
Noticed"Maria, after yesterday I noticed something: most denials are eligibility problems we only find at check-in, when
it's too late to fix them.
"
Idea
"What if CareBook ran the check automatically 72 hours before each visit, and texted the patient to update
their insurance if it fails?"
Impact"That targets about 75 denials a month at the pilot clinics — roughly 38 staﬀ hours and $6,750 in delayed
payments.
"
Eﬀort"It's about six working days, because we reuse the existing check and the reminder texts.
"
Test"We could start with video visits at the 3 pilot clinics for two weeks, and compare denial rates.
"
Ask"Would you support a two-week test? And is there any limit on how many checks we can run?"
Close
"I'll write it up on one page and share it with you and Dana by Thursday.
"
What good looks like: ✓ Used noticed → idea → impact → eﬀort ✓ Put the problem in dollars and hours ✓
Proposed a small first test ✓ Agreed a next step with a date

call.
"
WHO YOU ARE: [Your client's name, role and style.] If a field is still in [brackets], use the
CareBook story instead: you are Dana Brooks, VP of Product at Brightpath Health, and Kevin Walsh,
the CTO, joins the call — you may speak as Kevin, saying "Kevin here"
.
PROJECT CONTEXT: [2–3 lines about your real project — anonymised, no real patient data.] If this is
still in brackets, use the CareBook story instead: my company is the engineering partner of
Brightpath Health, a US telehealth company, building CareBook v2 (patient booking, SMS reminders,
intake forms, a clinic schedule view) synced with the clinic's Epic EHR. The launch customer is
Lakeside Health (12 clinics in Ohio). Everything is made up; no real patient data.
WHERE WE ARE NOW: [The situation before your real call.] CareBook fallback: Tuesday, May 19.
Tomorrow is the weekly sync after two weeks of pilot at 3 Lakeside clinics; the rollout to all 12
clinics is planned for June 15.
TODAY'S SCENARIO: I lead this 20-minute weekly sync. Let me open. Challenge me as a senior client:
ask for dates, push for a faster timeline, question the open bug. If I don't give a clear
recommendation, ask "So what do you recommend?"
HIDDEN FACTS — reveal each one only if my questions lead to it:
1. [Something your client is under pressure about.] CareBook fallback: Dana wants to announce the
rollout at Lakeside's board meeting on June 10.
2. [A question your client will save for the end.] CareBook fallback: the CEO has asked whether the
rollout can happen on June 8.
3. CareBook fallback: Kevin worries about SMS costs and load once all 12 clinics are live (about 4
times today's volume).
CURVEBALLS — use these during the call:
- Ask: "Can we do all 12 clinics on June 8 instead?"
- As Kevin: "What's the one thing that could break at 12 clinics?"
DIFFICULTY: Level 3 — sceptical; push back, interrupt at pauses, ask hard questions, apply time
pressure.
HOW TO BEHAVE
- Speak like a real American business person: natural speed, short sentences, everyday expressions.
- Keep each turn short (1–3 sentences), like a real call.
- Don't volunteer information; give details only when I ask good questions.
- If I use technical jargon, ask me what it means for your business.
- If I talk for more than 45 seconds without getting to the point, cut in at my next pause.
WHEN I SAY "End the call"
, step out of character and give me feedback:
1. Score me 1–5 on each criterion that came up: opening and agenda, answer-first structure,
questions asked, playback, business link, trade-offs, handling unknowns, risks and bad news,
disagreement, closing and next steps.
2. List the hidden facts and tell me which ones I uncovered.
3. Quote 2 things I said well, and 2 things I should have said differently — with the better
sentence.
4. Tell me roughly how often I used filler words ("um"
,
"basically"
,
"actually"
,
"like").
5. Give me ONE thing to focus on in my next call.

CURVEBALLS — use these during the call:
- Ask: "What's your weakest pillar, honestly?"
- Ask: "If I removed the PM from your project next week, what would go wrong first?"
DIFFICULTY: Level 2 — busy and vague; short answers, change topic, slightly impatient.
HOW TO BEHAVE
- Speak like a real American business person: natural speed, short sentences, everyday expressions.
- Keep each turn short (1–3 sentences), like a real call.
- Don't volunteer information; give details only when I ask good questions.
- If I use technical jargon, ask me what it means for your business.
- If I talk for more than 45 seconds without getting to the point, cut in at my next pause.
WHEN I SAY "End the call"
, step out of character and give me feedback:
1. Score me 1–5 on each criterion that came up: opening and agenda, answer-first structure,
questions asked, playback, business link, trade-offs, handling unknowns, risks and bad news,
disagreement, closing and next steps.
2. List the hidden facts and tell me which ones I uncovered.
3. Quote 2 things I said well, and 2 things I should have said differently — with the better
sentence.
4. Tell me roughly how often I used filler words ("um"
,
"basically"
,
"actually"
,
"like").
5. Give me ONE thing to focus on in my next call.
Real meeting like this? Before it, run the master prep prompt (Appendix B6) with Meeting type = "status update"
After it, run the master meeting-notes prompt (Appendix B7).
.
Client-Ready Engineer · 90-Day Upskilling Plan Page 149 of 198

the Pre-call card, filled in → 2. the call (a strong version) → 3. the written follow-up → 4. why it works, linked
to the call rubric (section 6). Use them as a reference for what "good" looks like — then practise the matching card.

safe.

You I can't guarantee it today. I'll know after Thursday's retest, and I'll update you on Thursday either way.
You I need your decision by noon tomorrow, and I'd like us to tell Dr. Mercer together on Thursday.
Dana Fine. Set up the call.

=== PAGE 163 ===
Appendix B — AI prompt library
Before you paste anything
No PHI, no client credentials, no confidential client documents in any AI tool the company hasn't approved.
Replace names and details with made-up ones.
B1. Master mock prompt
You are role-playing a US client on a video call with me. Stay in character until I say "End the
call.
"
WHO YOU ARE: [Persona, e.g.
"Dana Brooks, VP of Product at a telehealth startup in Austin, Texas"]
PROJECT CONTEXT: [2–3 lines about the project. Made-up or anonymised. No real patient data.]
TODAY'S SCENARIO: [Scenario from the library, e.g.
"S07 – You want 'a dashboard for your clinics'
but haven't thought through the details"]
DIFFICULTY: [Level 1 friendly / Level 2 busy and vague / Level 3 sceptical, interrupts, pushes back]
HOW TO BEHAVE
- Speak like a real American business person: natural speed, short sentences, everyday expressions.
- Don't volunteer information. Give details only when I ask good questions.
- If I use technical jargon, ask me what it means for your business.
- If I talk for more than 45 seconds without getting to the point, cut in at my next pause.
- Ask me at least one question I probably can't answer.
- Keep each of your turns short (1–3 sentences), like a real call.
WHEN I SAY "End the call"
, step out of character and give me feedback:
1. Score me 1–5 on each criterion that came up: opening and agenda, answer-first structure,
questions asked, playback, business link, trade-offs, handling unknowns, risks and bad news,
disagreement, closing and next steps.
2. Quote 2 things I said well, and 2 things I should have said differently — with the better
sentence.
3. Tell me roughly how often I used filler words ("um"
,
"basically"
,
"actually"
,
"like").
4. Give me ONE thing to focus on in my next call.
B2. Transcript feedback (Write block)
Here is the transcript of my practice call: [paste transcript]
Act as a communication coach for engineers who work directly with US clients.
Using the rubric below, score me 1–5 on each criterion that came up, with one sentence of evidence.
Then rewrite my 3 weakest answers the way a strong client-facing engineer would say them.
Keep your feedback short and practical.
Rubric: opening and agenda · answer-first structure · questions asked · playback · business link ·
trade-offs · handling unknowns · risks and bad news · disagreement · closing and next steps
B3. Pre-call prep (Prep block)
I have a [type of call] with [role] at a US healthcare company.
Context: [anonymised project context]. My goal for the call: [goal].
1. Suggest a 3-point agenda.
2. List the 8 questions they are most likely to ask me, including 2 hard ones.
3. List 3 risks or concerns I should raise proactively.
4. Give me a natural opening line and a closing line.
Keep it to one page.
Client-Ready Engineer · 90-Day Upskilling Plan Page 163 of 198

=== PAGE 164 ===
B4. Post-call recap
Here are my rough notes / the transcript from a client call (no PHI): [paste]
Turn them into a client-ready recap email with this structure:
Subject line (with the key decision or ask) · 2-line summary · Decisions · Action items (owner + due
date) ·
Open questions · Next meeting.
Use plain American business English, under 200 words.
Flag anything in my notes that is unclear, or that looks like a commitment I made.
Always edit before sending. You own the words, not the AI.
B5. Writing review
Review this message before I send it to a US client: [paste]
Check:
1. Is the main point or ask in the first two lines?
2. Is anything unclear, too long, or too indirect?
3. Are there phrases an American reader might misunderstand?
4. Is the tone confident but polite?
Give me a tighter version under [N] words, and list what you changed.
Client-Ready Engineer · 90-Day Upskilling Plan Page 164 of 198

=== PAGE 165 ===
B6. Master prep prompt — before a real client call
Use this before any real client meeting. Replace every [bracket] with your details — anonymised, never patient data.
Every mock card tells you which meeting type to use for a real meeting like it.
Master prep prompt — copy, fill in the brackets, paste
I have a client call coming up. Help me prepare. Use simple, natural American business English.
MEETING TYPE: [status update / discovery / bad news / disagreement / escalation / change request /
recommendation / demo / technical explanation / security or compliance questions / proposal]
CLIENT: [name, role, company — e.g.
"Dana, VP of Product at a US telehealth company"]
WHEN AND HOW LONG: [e.g.
"Friday 10:00 am Central, 15 minutes"]
MY GOAL FOR THIS CALL: [one sentence]
FACTS (anonymised — no patient data):
- Done: [ ]
- In progress: [ ]
- Delayed, and why: [ ]
- Risks: [ ]
- What I need from the client, and by when: [ ]
WHAT I KNOW ABOUT THEIR CONCERNS: [e.g.
"worried about the launch date"]
Give me, on one page:
1. A 3-point agenda and a warm opening line (small talk + purpose).
2. My main message, answer first, in 2–3 sentences.
3. The 5 questions they are most likely to ask — including 2 hard ones — with a short answer for
each.
If the answer is not in my facts, write "I'll check and confirm by [time]" instead of guessing.
4. The risks I should raise myself, before they ask.
5. The structure to follow for this meeting type
(for example, bad news: headline → impact → cause → options → what I need → next update).
6. A closing line that recaps decisions, owners and dates.
Flag anything in my facts that sounds vague, sounds like an excuse, or sounds like a promise I can't
keep.
B7. Master meeting-notes prompt — after the call
Use this within an hour of the call, so the recap reaches the client within 2 hours. Always read and edit the result
before you send it — you own the words, not the AI.
Master meeting-notes prompt — copy, fill in, paste your notes
Here are my rough notes (or the transcript) from a client call. They are anonymised — no patient
data.
MEETING: [type, client role, date and time with time zone]
NOTES: [paste]
Please give me:
1. Meeting notes: a 2-line summary · decisions · action items (owner + due date with time zone) ·
open questions · risks · next meeting.
2. A follow-up email to the client, ready to send: a subject line with the key decision or ask,
the bottom line in the first 2 lines, bullets, under 200 words, plain American business English.
3. Everything I committed to on the call, with the date I promised — so I don't miss one.
4. Anything in my notes that is unclear, contradicts itself, or is missing an owner or a date.
5. A quick coach's view: score me 1–5 on answer-first, questions and playback, handling unknowns,
and closing with next steps — one line of evidence each — and one thing to do better next time.
Client-Ready Engineer · 90-Day Upskilling Plan Page 165 of 198

B8. Worked example — B6 and B7 end to end
A real-style example based on the S04 weekly sync with Dana (CareBook practice project): the prep prompt filled in,
what the AI gives back, the rough notes after the call, and the finished meeting notes and email.
Step 1 — Before the call: B6 with the brackets filled in
MEETING TYPE: status update (weekly sync)
CLIENT: Dana, VP of Product at a US telehealth company
WHEN AND HOW LONG: Friday March 6, 10:00 am Central, 15 minutes
MY GOAL FOR THIS CALL: Give a clear weekly update, raise the Epic access risk, and get a name
for the security-form signer.
FACTS:
- Done: video booking at 75% (was 60%). Dana approved the SMS reminder wording on Mar 5;
it is with Lakeside compliance.
- In progress: reschedule/cancel (Mar 9–11). SMS reminders are waiting on SMS number
verification (submitted Mar 3; usually 5–7 business days).
- Delayed, and why: nothing late yet.
- Risks: Epic sandbox access. Lakeside IT needs a signed security form first; it is not signed.
We need access by Mon Mar 16.
- What I need from the client: who at Brightpath signs Lakeside's security form, by Tue Mar 10.
WHAT I KNOW ABOUT THEIR CONCERNS: the pilot date, and next Friday's Sprint 3 demo.

next week's plan — 15 minutes.

reminder wording is with Lakeside compliance.

=== PAGE 168 ===
Appendix C — Healthcare glossary
Plain-English definitions. The goal is business-level understanding, not expert detail.
The US healthcare system
Term Meaning
Provider Payer Medicare Medicaid HHS CMS ONC Fee-for-service (FFS) Value-based care (VBC) ACO Anyone who delivers care: hospitals, clinics, doctors, nurses.
Whoever pays for care: private insurers, Medicare, Medicaid, employers.
Federal health insurance, mainly for people aged 65 and over.
Joint federal and state program for people with low incomes.
US Department of Health and Human Services — the parent agency of CMS, ONC and OCR.
Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services. Runs Medicare and Medicaid and sets many
payment rules.
The federal oﬃce for health IT and interoperability rules (now part of ASTP/ONC).
Providers are paid for each service they perform.
Providers are paid for outcomes and quality, not volume.
Accountable Care Organization: a group of providers that shares responsibility (and savings) for
a patient population.
Privacy and security
Term Meaning
HIPAA PHI / ePHI Covered entity Business associate BAA Minimum necessary De-identified data Audit log SOC 2 / HITRUST The 1996 US law that protects the privacy and security of health information.
Protected Health Information: health information that can identify a person. ePHI is PHI in
electronic form.
Providers, payers and clearinghouses that must follow HIPAA.
A vendor that handles PHI on behalf of a covered entity — often us.
Business Associate Agreement: the contract that allows a vendor to handle PHI.
Use or share only the PHI needed for the task.
Data with identifiers removed, so it is no longer PHI.
A record of who accessed which data, and when.
Security certifications that healthcare clients often ask vendors about.
Clinical systems and interoperability
Term Meaning
EHR / EMR Epic, Oracle Health (Cerner),
athenahealth
MRN Electronic Health Record / Electronic Medical Record — the digital patient chart.
Major EHR vendors in the US.
Medical Record Number — the patient ID inside a hospital's system.
Client-Ready Engineer · 90-Day Upskilling Plan Page 168 of 198

=== PAGE 169 ===
Term Meaning
Encounter ADT CMIO Interoperability HL7 v2 FHIR FHIR resource SMART on FHIR C-CDA Any patient interaction: a visit, an admission, a telehealth session.
Admit, Discharge, Transfer — events (and HL7 messages) when a patient moves through a
hospital.
Chief Medical Information Oﬃcer — a doctor who leads clinical IT.
Systems exchanging data and being able to use it.
An older, very widely used messaging standard between hospital systems.
Fast Healthcare Interoperability Resources — the modern API standard (REST + JSON).
A unit of FHIR data, such as Patient, Appointment, Observation, or MedicationRequest.
A standard that lets apps launch inside an EHR with secure login (OAuth).
A standard clinical document format, e.g. a visit summary.
Information blocking Practices that unreasonably stop health data being shared; restricted under the 21st Century
Cures Act.
Patient apps and telehealth
Term Meaning
Telehealth Care delivered remotely: video, phone, or messaging.
RPM Remote Patient Monitoring: devices (blood-pressure cuﬀs, glucose meters) that send readings
to care teams.
Patient portal A website or app where patients see records, book visits, and message their care team.
No-show rate The percentage of booked appointments that patients miss.
Patient engagement How actively patients use digital tools and follow their care plans.
TCPA US law on automated calls and texts — patients must consent to SMS reminders.
WCAG Web Content Accessibility Guidelines — accessibility standards for web and app content.
Billing and revenue cycle (RCM)
Term Meaning
RCM Revenue Cycle Management: the whole process of getting paid for care.
Eligibility verification Checking that the patient's insurance is active and what it covers.
Prior authorization Payer approval required before certain treatments or services.
ICD-10 Codes for diagnoses — what's wrong with the patient.
CPT Codes for procedures and services — what was done.
HCPCS Codes for supplies, equipment and some services, used heavily by Medicare.
Claim The bill sent to the payer.
Clearinghouse A middleman that checks claims and forwards them to payers.
X12 837 / 835 Electronic standard formats for claims (837) and for payment and remittance details (835).
Client-Ready Engineer · 90-Day Upskilling Plan Page 169 of 198

=== PAGE 170 ===
Term Meaning
EOB / ERA Denial Denial rate Days in A/R Clean-claim rate Copay / deductible /
coinsurance
Explanation of Benefits (sent to the patient) / Electronic Remittance Advice (sent to the
provider).
The payer refuses to pay a claim — for example, missing information or a wrong code.
The percentage of claims denied. A key RCM metric.
The average number of days it takes to get paid (accounts receivable).
The percentage of claims accepted the first time they are submitted.
The patient's share of costs: a fixed fee / the amount paid before insurance starts / a
percentage share.

=== PAGE 175 ===
MOCK CARD X01 · OPTIONAL EXTRA PRACTICE
Standup: a claims/eligibility API ticket slipped a day — give a 90-second
update
Dana Brooks (P1), VP of Product, Brightpath · Level 2 · 10 min · Skill: Update framework
The story so far. It's Tuesday, May 19, two weeks into the pilot. The daily standup with Dana is at 9:00 am ET (6:30
pm IST). Your ticket — the pre-visit eligibility check for the 9 rollout clinics — was due yesterday and isn't done.
Your fact sheet Your ticket What happened Impact What you're doing What you need Timebox What you know before the call
CB-412: run a Suncoast eligibility check 72 hours before every visit at the 9 rollout clinics. Due Mon May
18. About 85% done.
Suncoast returns a status code ("inactive – pending review") that isn't in their docs. 31 of 500 test checks
fail on it. Found Monday afternoon.
Ready Wed May 20 instead of Mon May 18 — one day late. The Jun 15 rollout is not aﬀected. QA loses
one of three test days.
Our tech lead emailed Suncoast support Monday at 4 pm ET. Meanwhile, the unknown code shows as
"needs manual check" so the front desk sees it.
Dana to confirm by end of today that "needs manual check" is OK for the pilot until Suncoast answers.
90 seconds. Dana has 8 more updates to hear.
Your goal: Give a 90-second update in the order Context → What happened → Impact → What I'm doing → What I
need, and get a yes or no on your ask.
What the client may throw at you: "So is the rollout at risk or not?"
·
"Can't we just ignore that code for now?"
·
"Why didn't we know about this code earlier?"
Step Example script — use it the first time, then try without it
Context"Quick update on the pre-visit eligibility check for the 9 rollout clinics.
"
What happened"It slipped one day. Suncoast is sending a status code that isn't in their documentation — 31 of our 500 test
checks hit it.
"
Impact"It'll be ready tomorrow, Wednesday, instead of yesterday. June 15 is not aﬀected; QA loses one test day.
"
Doing"Our tech lead raised it with Suncoast yesterday. Until they reply, we flag that code as 'needs manual check' so
the front desk sees it.
"
Need"One thing from you: are you OK with 'needs manual check' for now? A yes by end of today keeps us on
track.
"
Close
"I'll post the same update in Slack right after this call.
"
What good looks like: ✓ 5-part update in under 90 seconds ✓ Said clearly that June 15 is not aﬀected ✓ One
specific ask with a deadline ✓ No long apology or excuse
Next page: the AI block. Copy it into Claude or ChatGPT as text, switch to voice mode, and speak first.

Kevin Walsh (P4), CTO, Brightpath · Level 2 · 15–20 min · Skill: Bad news in writing
The story so far. It's Thursday, May 21. Suncoast's new eligibility API v2 was due on Monday and still isn't
available. Write the email to Kevin before the 10:00 am ET call (10 minutes), then defend it live for 5 minutes.
Your fact sheet The delay Impact If it slips again Option A Option B What you know before the call
Suncoast's eligibility API v2 (needed for insurance plans at the 9 new clinics that v1 doesn't cover) was
promised Mon May 18. New date: Thu May 21 — 3 days late, not yet in writing.
We need 4 working days after it arrives. If it lands Thursday, we finish Wed May 27 — 2 days of buﬀer
before rollout testing starts Fri May 29.
Every extra day comes out of testing for the Jun 15 rollout. After Tue May 26, the rollout date is at risk.
Wait for v2. Keeps the design clean. Risk: no buﬀer if Suncoast slips again.
Build on the current v1 API now for the 80% of plans it covers; add v2 later. About 2 extra days of work.
Protects Jun 15.
Recommendation Start Option B today as insurance; drop it if v2 arrives by Tue May 26. Need Kevin's OK by 2:00 pm ET
today.
Your goal: Send an email with the bad news and the ask in the first 2 lines, then defend it on the call and leave with
a decision on Option B.
What the client may throw at you: "Why are we paying for double work?"
"Can Suncoast give us a firm date?"
·
"Is this Suncoast's fault or ours?"
·
Step Example script — use it the first time, then try without it
Email line 1"Subject: Suncoast eligibility v2 is 3 days late — decision needed by 2 pm ET. Bottom line: Suncoast has
moved the API from Monday to Thursday.
"
Email line 2"If it slips past Tuesday, June 15 is at risk. I recommend we start a fallback on the current API today.
"
Call: open
"Thanks for making time, Kevin. You've seen the email — I'd like 5 minutes to walk through the options and get
a decision.
"
Options
"Option A: we wait — clean, but no buﬀer. Option B: we build on the current API now for 80% of plans — about
2 extra days, but June 15 is protected.
"
Recommend"I recommend B as insurance. If v2 arrives by Tuesday, we drop the fallback.
"
Close
"Can I have your OK on B today? I'll confirm in writing and update you Tuesday either way.
"
What good looks like: ✓ Bad news and the ask in the email's first 2 lines ✓ Two options with clear trade-oﬀs ✓ A
recommendation with a date to drop the fallback ✓ No blame; left with a decision

Jordan Lee, Support Lead, Brightpath · Level 2 · 10 min · Skill: Stand firm on safety
The story so far. It's Tuesday, June 2. Jordan Lee, Brightpath's Support Lead, has asked for a quick call. Support is
overwhelmed with booking tickets from the pilot clinics, and Jordan has an idea to "speed things up"
.
Your fact sheet The request Facts on SSNs Our rule Real pain Safer option Decision owner What you know before the call
Jordan wants full patient details — including Social Security numbers and insurance member IDs —
written into CareBook's logs "so support can find people faster"
.
CareBook doesn't collect SSNs at all. It stores insurance member IDs, which are PHI.
No PHI in logs. HIPAA "minimum necessary": use only the PHI you need. Logs get copied into many tools
and seen by many people.
Support gets about 60 booking tickets a week; finding the right patient takes about 12 minutes per ticket.
A support lookup screen: search by booking reference, masked member ID (last 4 digits), role-based
access, every lookup in the audit log. About 4 days of work.
Kevin Walsh (CTO) owns security decisions.
Your goal: Say no to PHI in logs clearly and calmly, show you understand Jordan's real problem, and agree a safer
next step.
What the client may throw at you: "Other companies do this all the time.
would see them?"
·
"How long will your option take?"
"
·
"It's only our internal logs — who
Step Open
Acknowledge
Concern
Alternative
Example script — use it the first time, then try without it
"Thanks, Jordan. I hear the team is buried in booking tickets — let's fix that. What slows you down most on
each ticket?"
"So finding the right patient takes about 12 minutes a ticket. That's real pain.
"
"My concern with putting member IDs or SSNs in logs is that logs get copied everywhere. If they leak, that's a
privacy incident for Lakeside, not just for us.
"
"Instead, we could give support a lookup screen: search by booking reference, show only the last 4 digits, and
record every lookup. About 4 days of work.
"
Ask"Can we take this to Kevin together this week? Security decisions are Kevin's call.
"
Close
"I'll write a short summary of both ideas today so Kevin has it before we meet.
"

Oﬀered a safer option with an eﬀort estimate ✓ Named Kevin as decision owner and set a next step

MOCK CARD X09 · OPTIONAL EXTRA PRACTICE
Correct a teammate who says "HIPAA prevents all logging"
A junior engineer on our team · Level 1 · 10 min · Skill: Correct without humiliating
The story so far. It's Friday, May 22. A junior engineer who joined the team this week, says on a quick call: "HIPAA
prevents all logging, so let's turn oﬀ logs in the booking service.
" You have 5 minutes to correct this without making
them feel small.
Your fact sheet The claim The principle What we log Audit logs Who confirms What you know before the call
The junior engineer believes HIPAA bans logging. It doesn't.
Protect PHI and use only the minimum necessary. Logs are fine when they contain no PHI.
Booking IDs, error codes, timings and service names. Never names, dates of birth, phone numbers,
member IDs or visit reasons.
HIPAA's Security Rule expects audit controls — records of who accessed which data. CareBook keeps
these in a separate, access-controlled audit log.
Kevin Walsh (CTO) and the compliance team, for anything unclear.
Your goal: Correct the misunderstanding in under 3 minutes, explain the principle simply, and point them to who
can confirm.
What the client may throw at you: "So can we log the patient's name if it helps debugging?"
what counts as PHI?"
·
"Why do we need audit logs at all?"
·
"How do I know
Step Example script — use it the first time, then try without it
Open
"Good instinct to be careful with patient data — that matters a lot here.
"
Correct"HIPAA doesn't stop us logging. It asks us to protect PHI and use only what we need.
"
Practice
"So we log booking IDs, error codes and timings — never names, birth dates, phone numbers or member IDs.
Audit"We actually need some logs: the audit log records who looked at patient data. That's expected for security.
"
Confirm
"If you're ever unsure, ask Kevin or the compliance team before you log something.
"
Close
"Want to pair for 15 minutes tomorrow and review the booking service logs together?"
"
What good looks like: ✓ Corrected it kindly; the teammate didn't feel small ✓ Explained "protect PHI, minimum
necessary" in plain words ✓ Gave concrete examples of what to log and not log ✓ Pointed to who confirms, and
oﬀered help

MOCK CARD X11 · OPTIONAL EXTRA PRACTICE
Batch vs streaming eligibility checks — present options and recommend one
Kevin Walsh (P4), CTO, Brightpath · Level 3 · 15–20 min · Skill: Recommend: options + trade-oﬀs
The story so far. It's Wednesday, June 10. Dana wants insurance eligibility to be "instant" for every booking at all 12
clinics. Kevin Walsh has asked you to present the options and your recommendation before the June 15 rollout.
Your fact sheet Volume A: real-time B: nightly batch C: hybrid Compliance What you know before the call
About 42,000 eligibility checks a month across 12 clinics.
Check Suncoast at every booking. Instant for patients. $0.10 a check ≈ $4,200 a month. Suncoast limit:
20 requests a second — busy mornings get close.
Check all visits 72 hours ahead in one nightly file. $0.03 a check ≈ $1,260 a month. Not instant; misses
same-day bookings.
Nightly batch 72 hours ahead + real-time only for same-day bookings and failed checks. ≈ $1,900 a
month. Covers every visit.
All options send the same minimum data. Results are kept 24 hours, then refreshed.
Your recommendation Option C, with a fallback if Suncoast is down: the booking goes through and shows "needs manual
check" for the front desk.
Your goal: Present three options that diﬀer on speed, cost, risk and failure handling, recommend one, say what you
would NOT do, and land a decision with an owner and date.
What the client may throw at you: "Why not just do real-time? It's simpler.
down?"
·
"Who told you 20 requests a second?"
"
·
"What happens when Suncoast is
Step Example script — use it the first time, then try without it
Situation
"Dana wants eligibility to feel instant at booking. At 12 clinics that's about 42,000 checks a month.
"
Options
"Option A: real-time on every booking — instant, but about $4,200 a month and close to Suncoast's rate limit.
Option B: a nightly batch — about $1,260, but it misses same-day bookings.
"
Option C"Option C is a hybrid: the nightly batch plus real-time only for same-day bookings and failures — about $1,900
a month.
"
Recommend"I recommend C. I would not do A — it puts us near the rate limit on Monday mornings.
"
Failure plan
"If Suncoast is down, the booking still goes through and the front desk sees 'needs manual check'
"
.
Decision
"Can you approve C today? Our tech lead can have it ready by Friday, June 12, and I'll write up the decision.
"
What good looks like: ✓ Options diﬀer on speed, cost, risk and failure handling ✓ Clear recommendation, plus
what you would NOT do ✓ Handled pushback with facts, not defensiveness ✓ Decision with owner and date; written
record oﬀered

1. Suncoast had a 2-hour outage last month.
2. You would accept the hybrid if the failure handling is clear.
3. Tom Reyes (CFO) asked you to keep new monthly costs under $2,500.
4. You want the decision written down as a short decision record.
CURVEBALLS — use these during the call:
- Ask: "Who told you 20 requests a second — is that in writing?"
- Say: "Real-time is simpler to build. Why add a batch job?"
DIFFICULTY: Level 3 — sceptical; push back, interrupt at pauses, ask hard questions, apply time
pressure.
HOW TO BEHAVE
- Speak like a real American business person: natural speed, short sentences, everyday expressions.
- Keep each turn short (1–3 sentences), like a real call.
- Don't volunteer information; give details only when I ask good questions.
- If I use technical jargon, ask me what it means for your business.
- If I talk for more than 45 seconds without getting to the point, cut in at my next pause.
WHEN I SAY "End the call"
, step out of character and give me feedback:
1. Score me 1–5 on each criterion that came up: opening and agenda, answer-first structure,
questions asked, playback, business link, trade-offs, handling unknowns, risks and bad news,
disagreement, closing and next steps.
2. List the hidden facts and tell me which ones I uncovered.
3. Quote 2 things I said well, and 2 things I should have said differently — with the better
sentence.
4. Tell me roughly how often I used filler words ("um"
,
"basically"
,
"actually"
,
"like").
5. Give me ONE thing to focus on in my next call.

Your fact sheet Mapping bugs Consent wording Sales promise Options Unsafe shortcut What you know before the call
3% of Epic appointment types from the 9 new clinics don't map to CareBook visit types (about 40
appointments a day). Fix: 3 days, plus 2 days of testing.
Lakeside's legal team hasn't approved the Spanish SMS consent text. Without it, Spanish reminders can't
go out. Approval expected Jun 10 — not confirmed.
Megan Ross (Sales) told Lakeside a custom "provider worklist" would be live at rollout. Engineering never
agreed; it's about 2 weeks of work.
(A) All 12 clinics on Jun 15 with a fallback for unmapped types. (B) 9 clinics on Jun 15, the last 3 on Jun
22. Both: English reminders on day 1, Spanish once approved.
Skipping mapping validation to save time — unmapped appointments could show patients the wrong visit

Step Example script — use it the first time, then try without it
Open

consent wording, and the provider worklist.
"
Status
"Overall status: Yellow. We can go live, but we need two decisions today.
"
Mapping"3% of appointment types from the new clinics don't map yet. Option A: all 12 clinics with a fallback. Option B:
9 clinics on the 15th and the last 3 on the 22nd. I recommend B.
"
Disagree
"I understand the wish to skip mapping validation. My concern is patients seeing the wrong visit type. I'd rather
go live with 9 clinics safely than 12 with that risk.
"
Consent"English reminders go out on day 1; Spanish once legal approves. Dr. Mercer, could you help us get that
approval by June 10?"
Worklist"The provider worklist wasn't in scope. It's about 2 weeks of work — Dana, can we agree a date after the
rollout?"
Close

notes within 2 hours.
"

shortcut, with an alternative ✓ Every decision had an owner and a date ✓ Clear recap on the call; notes promised
within 2 hours

WHO YOU ARE: Three people — play all of them and label who is speaking. Dana Brooks, VP of Product

checks through Suncoast Billing. The launch customer is Lakeside Health (12 primary-care clinics in

Sales has promised Lakeside a custom provider worklist at rollout.

3. Dana is under board pressure but would rather be safe than have a public incident.
4. Lakeside legal told Dr. Mercer approval will come on June 10, but hasn't told Brightpath yet.
5. Megan Ross promised the worklist on a sales call; there is nothing in writing.

- Dana: "Why wasn't the worklist in the plan? Lakeside thinks it's coming.
